# Server protocol and transfers

*What rides directly on the authenticated SSH connection for the whole of a machine's life; how a request is framed, versioned, negotiated, correlated and submitted so that sending it twice does nothing twice; what makes a command admissible before any Smoozy component exists on the machine; where the authentication boundary sits and what may never be keyed on a machine's remembered identity; how the bootstrap-and-repair request reaches a machine whose supervisor is dead or that has never held a package; how transfer data moves on channels Smoozy reads and writes itself; the one staging, integrity, resume, publication and cleanup contract that holds in both directions and does not change at hand-over; how a per-channel rate cap is applied and how it reaches a transfer already running; at what moment a free-space reading is taken and how far it may be trusted; and the rule that no piece of work is carried out with nothing to report until it is done.*

*2026-10-27 — Removed the withdrawn paid-access text and its obsolete references under the Creator’s free-and-open-source decision of 2026-10-04 (wave 5); retained protections and evidence keep their force. No auditor has seen this removal.*

Rule 1's plain-English rule is suspended here, per the Architecture stage's exception.

**Scope, from [The shape](The%20shape.md#4-server-protocol-and-transfers):** the pre-package SSH surface for the machine's whole life; the versioned bootstrap-and-repair request and package inspection; the versioned SSH command protocol and request framing over the machine-local endpoint; request correlation; authentication boundary; capability negotiation; idempotent submission; atomic acknowledgement; resuming correlation across a connection Servers replaced; re-establishing transfer channels; cancellation; per-waiter deadlines; independent SSH transfer-data channels; upload and download staging, integrity, resume, publication, staging cleanup, per-channel rate enforcement, concurrency, and transfer priority. `Servers` owns when a connection is made or replaced. `Rented-server runtime` owns the machine-local endpoint and every machine-side response to these requests. This document takes identities and state transitions from `State, identity and recovery`, connects the desktop application to `Rented-server runtime`, and supplies pre-package requests and transfer primitives to `File Browser`, `Dependency Upload & Remap`, `Download`, `Server Setup`, `Render Settings`, `Rendering` and `Queue & History`.

**File reading's library pairs, added 2026-11-06:** `Render Settings` Q4's file-inspection request optionally carries the saved library pairs as JSON in a field named `the reading's library pairs`. The supervisor validates them, records them with the operation, and hands them unchanged to its worker. Absent means no pairs, preserving older callers. These pairs only construct `Dependency Upload & Remap` M8b's opening folder; they do not rewrite the file or apply the remap material.

## What binds this before anything is decided

**`State, identity and recovery` is the input this document leans on hardest, and its idempotency and identity rules are not restated as a second set.** [Section 4](#4-correlation-idempotent-submission-and-atomic-acknowledgement) is that document's [§12](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) four rules applied to the protocol, and nothing here adds a value beside the operation identity. Its [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) names the **staged-transfer area** as this document's: the material is this document's, the state is that one's.

**Fact 78 is answered on ext4, xfs and overlayfs, and Fact 79's documentation route is answered on all three user systems; only the bounded residuals remain.** Fact 78's two-file half is **answered and negative** — *"no single operation updates two independent files atomically"* — while real forcible kills at every commit boundary on **ext4, xfs and overlayfs** left no part-arrived bytes at a final name and always left the pointer naming exactly one version ([The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §8). What remains is an interrupted rollback, a filesystem outside those three, and Fact 44's genuine power cut. Fact 79's documentation route is answered positively on Mac and Linux and negatively on Windows; its per-system throwaway route remains unrun. [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#96-where-a-unit-must-be-atomic-and-the-ground-for-it-is-not-established) §9.6 already says what is done: **the unit is written as atomic and the document resting on it says so**, rather than the rule being weakened to fit. That is taken and not decided again; [Section 12](#12-unresolved) items 1 and 2 state what remains and what breaks.

**A host key names neither the rental nor the installation, so nothing here may be keyed on one.** Fact 83 is answered negatively, and **more widely than this clause used to state — corrected 2026-08-22**. A fresh rental on the same physical machine presented **byte-identical** keys while being a demonstrably different container with everything Smoozy installed destroyed — *"The host key survived the rental it belonged to"* ([The host key](The%20host%20key%20—%20obtained%20without%20authenticating,%20and%20whose%20it%20turns%20out%20to%20be.md), Fact 83). **This used to say the keys are the container image's, baked in at build time, and that a virtual machine differs because it *"generated its own host keys at first boot"*. Both halves are disproved.** A container's keys are **the host's** — the same offer re-rented gave three identical keys, a different machine on the same image gave three different ones ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md) Test 19) — and the virtual machine observed was **shipped** its keys in the image, files dated months before the rental and byte-identical after a recycle ([The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §17). **So on neither kind does an unchanged host key mean the machine is the same machine, and the prohibition below is strengthened rather than qualified.** **No correlation, no resumption of a transfer or a command, and no trust decision in this document is keyed on a host key.** What tells a rental from an installation is the **installation identity** of [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#23-the-machines-installation-identity) §2.3, and [4.5](#45-a-reconnection-is-gated-on-the-installation-identity-and-not-on-reachability) is that gate applied here. Row 36's changed-identity warning is `Servers`'.

**A container rental's recycle destroys everything Smoozy installed and comes back on the same address and port with nothing in the connection to reveal it**, in about 90 seconds against 15–20 for a reboot, with the boot hook *"not deleted but replaced"* by an 82-byte provider default — so *"a check for 'does my hook exist' passes while the hook does nothing Smoozy asked for"* ([Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), *Recycle*). Every piece of protocol state this document carries across a reconnection is either destroyed by that case or says what it does with it.

**A webhook is a provider-dependent extra rather than something Smoozy can rely on**, and the ordinary case is that **no rented server binds an inbound endpoint at all** — **a URL naming a Vast container's own mapped port is refused because the provider chooses the number, and a Vast virtual machine was not tried** (narrowed 2026-08-22 — this said *a Vast container rental cannot host one whatever it is rented as*, which reads a fact about one address as a fact about a machine) ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#13-the-inbound-boundary-at-a-rented-server) §1.3). **Nothing in this document hardens that boundary in either direction.** Every request here is made by the desktop application over a connection it opened outward; nothing in this document requires a rented server to accept an inbound connection or to call the user's computer.

**Nothing in this document is derived from a reading of the user’s computer.**

**Three operating systems on the user's end, Linux alone on the rented end**, which is Row 282 and `Desktop app and delivery`'s. Every answer below that touches the user's own computer is three answers. **Windows throwaways are permanently unresolved, 2026-09-02 — this read *have not been posed, no Windows machine having been rented for them*, which is a shortage that could end, and it cannot.** This project has no Windows computer and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be **no further rented machine for answering a question**, so no Windows throwaway is ever run: **not a pending item, and no reader may leave a space in the code or in the record for one.** **Documentation questions remain posable by reading**, on any machine, and that is the route every Windows answer below takes. Where an answer below is written for two systems and not the third, it says so.

**Do not read past the input documents.** Every machine observation quoted below is a frozen snapshot of one or two boxes on one or two days, in those documents' own words: *"Read it as *this is what that box did*, never as *this is what a rented machine does*."*

**What the observations already say about transfers, and none of it is softened below.**

- A killed or dropped copy leaves bytes **at the final name** with *"identical mode, permissions, extended attributes, inode flags, allocation and file-type output"* to a whole file, in both directions, and an interrupted **overwrite** left a *"**40,000,000-byte hybrid** — 10,444,800 new bytes spliced onto 29,555,200 old ones. Neither version, plausible size, no marker"* ([Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 18; [The rented machine](The%20rented%20machine.md)). **So anything relying on the size looking wrong is defeated.**
- Damage in the pipe is caught hard but **presents as a dropped connection** — `message authentication code incorrect` downloading, `Broken pipe` uploading — *"indistinguishable from an ordinary network drop without reading the exit text"*, it leaves the same nameless partial at the final name, and *"The machine's own log said **nothing at all**"* (Fact 72).
- **Damage at rest is invisible to both ordinary tools** unless a comparison is asked for: four bytes overwritten in place with the modification time put back travelled through `scp` at exit 0, and a default `rsync` *"called it done: 48 bytes sent, exit 0, file still corrupted"* (Fact 72).
- **The partial-holding directory cannot be relied on after a hard kill** — one box left rsync's own *"randomly named temporary file"* whose name Smoozy cannot predict — while it is reliable *"after an interruption it performs itself"* (Fact 17).
- A total rate cap holds across machines, but **per-invocation caps forfeit the budget the moment one transfer finishes** (421.9 KB/s achieved against an 800 KB/s budget split 400+400), while one shared budget in one process achieved **820 KB/s** and cut 21.85 s to 11.24. **The shared-budget mechanism was exercised on uploads only** (Fact 75).
- The connection is **11–12 times faster down than up** (12–15 MB/s down, 1.2–1.3 up); the useful concurrency range against one machine is **two to four streams**; a whole second rented machine bought about **20%, not 100%**; and with three machines running *"the slowest set the finish time"* — 112 seconds for a job whose fast half was done in 28. Uncoordinated upload fan-out was **actively worse** than one machine at a time: 1.54, 1.24, 0.99 MB/s across one, two and three boxes (Fact 25).
- A file on the server can be told not to have changed **from size, inode, modification time and change time together**; a save-by-rename changes the inode; and **a hand-restored modification time still moves the change time, which cannot be set back** (Fact 21).
- Free space *"is not a reading, it is a choice of which mount to read"*: a plain reading on one container returned **51 filesystems**, 48 of them memory-backed, and of the three that were not, one was *"the **host's** disk, bind-mounted in to deliver the driver files"* showing 20 GiB free the rental may not use (Fact 28).

## 1. The pre-package SSH surface

**Constraint:** [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) — *"The desktop application uses a pre-package SSH surface carried directly over the authenticated SSH connection. Before the server package exists, no Smoozy component is installed on the machine. The surface remains available for the whole of the machine's life."*

### 1.1 What the surface is made of, and it is two mechanisms rather than one

The surface is carried on the connection `Servers` holds, and it is made of exactly two mechanisms. **A builder needs both named, because they have different framing, different failure text and different capability questions.**

1. **The command channel.** One SSH `exec` request carries one **command string**, returning output, complaint output and exit status. RFC 4254 §6.5 defines that request as a string named `command`; it does not provide an argument vector. **So no user path, package path, option, identifier or other value of user, provider or machine origin is ever interpolated into a command string — in either phase — and the two phases differ only in what the constant string is.** **After hand-over** Smoozy sends one constant, installation-owned command — the active package's fixed entry-point name ([B8](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection))~~, at the installation-owned location whose layout is `Rented-server runtime`'s~~ — and sends every value on that entry point's standard input in this document's length-delimited frame. **Corrected 2026-09-29; found by building it at Build step 8b and carried on the Build board since 2026-09-07. The entry point's own path is not constant, so the command cannot name it where it stands.** It sits in the package area, and where the package area sits is `Job files and outputs` L4a's — chosen once per installation from candidates read off the machine — which is a machine-origin value this section forbids in a command string. **So the entry point is reached by the rule's own planted-link carriage, after hand-over as before it**: a link whose name is a fixed token is planted pointing at the package area, the constant command names the fixed entry-point name under that token, ~~and the link is taken away when the ask ends.~~ **and the link stands between the asks made on the connection that planted it**, so an ask that finds it standing is one command and no file-transfer request. **It is planted again wherever it may not stand**: on every new connection, after every act that takes the whole connection (a placement plants and takes away a link at this site itself), after an ask whose command did not end cleanly (a link someone removed answers with no program), and for another package area; and the plant replaces whatever stands at the token first, as it always has, so a link an ended connection left on the machine is replaced by the next connection's first ask. **Asks still take turns on one connection**, because a link being planted or replaced is a link another ask's command could miss. *Changed 2026-10-26 by the builder of the worldwide pass's setup faults (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/Brief — the worldwide pass's setup faults.md`), a hole found by building it: planting and taking away the link on every ask cost four round trips of the line beside a command of about two, so on a server 300 ms away each way every ask was about 3.6 seconds and `Server Setup` Y7d's one-second refresh could not be kept — the worldwide pass on Vast watched the setup card's words arrive in batches every 3 to 12 seconds. With the link standing, the same ask at the desk is about 1.2 seconds. The link is Smoozy's own token pointing at Smoozy's own package area, which does not move for an installation, so a link left standing claims nothing. No auditor has seen it.* **The link points at the package area and not at the entry point**, because the machine's program takes its package area from the directory it was invoked from; a link planted at the entry point itself would hand it the carriage directory instead. The command string is still one constant, and every value still rides standard input. That entry carries the SSH command adapter, and with it readiness, hardware readings, digest computation over a byte range, and every request the second mechanism has no operation for. **The entry point is not the only constant string the channel carries after hand-over — corrected 2026-08-31, at the first audited reading of this document against [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md); this sentence read as exclusive and two documents' post-hand-over work is not the entry point.** The channel also carries the **admissible constant commands** of the rule below, which is what `Server Setup`'s elevated steps and `File Browser`'s elevated readings and file operations are, and **the admissibility rule and its carriage routes bind in both phases** rather than before hand-over only. What hand-over changes is that the entry point exists and answers framed requests; it does not make an admissible command inadmissible, and no mechanism of the rule changes with the phase. The entry point never passes a framed path through a shell. Where it invokes a program, it constructs the argument vector itself, uses the program's end-of-options delimiter where the program supports one, and otherwise uses a fixed non-option operand route; a leading dash, whitespace, a newline and a non-UTF-8 filename are values rather than syntax. **Before hand-over there is no Smoozy program on the machine and none is required — no wrapper, no bootstrap, no placed component of any kind.** The command channel then carries only **admissible commands** under the rule at the end of this section: constant strings naming programs the machine already carries, with every value carried off the command string. What that phase actually needs the channel for is short — hardware readings, `Servers` Q12's operating-system reading, `Server Setup`'s package, driver, boot-registration, restart and elevated steps, and the elevated retries `File Browser` owns — and every one of them is either constant or carries its value by one of the rule's routes — the boot-registration lingering grant and its read-back by the fourth, machine self-derivation, whose command strings are constant and whose one value never leaves the machine. Package inspection is on neither list, because it is a composed subsystem reading in both phases ([B4](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection)); the at-rest digest is the framed request after hand-over and a subsystem read-back before it ([S22](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)).
2. **The file-transfer subsystem channel.** The standard SSH file-transfer subsystem, which is what carries directory listing, file attributes, file operations, free-space readings, and every byte of every transfer. **Its operations are byte-offset reads and writes against an open handle**, which is what lets Smoozy own the byte loop rather than hand a whole file to a tool. **The reading traffic normally holds one long-lived subsystem channel on the primary connection** — listings, attributes and free-space readings alike — opened at the first reading and held for the connected period, rather than one channel opened and closed per reading; each transfer opens its own channel beside it ([T1](#61-a-transfer-is-a-channel-and-the-channel-is-independent-of-the-command-channel)). At a one-session cap, [T5](#62-concurrency-and-one-admission-point-rather-than-one-per-server)'s primary lease closes that reading channel for a command and reopens it afterwards. **Every one of those channels is a session on the connection and is admitted and counted under T5**, as is every command invocation of item 1.

   **Its operations are four rather than three, and they are ordered. Added 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, routed by the runner from [File Browser](File%20Browser.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 12 and [Download](Download.md#15-unresolved) §15 item 22.** Beside listings, attributes and free-space readings, the long-lived reading channel also carries **a byte-offset read against an open handle taken for something other than a transfer** — `File Browser`'s prefix read is the standing instance, and a transfer's own bytes are not this, because a transfer opens its own channel. **The ordering, which nothing supplied before:** operations on this channel are served **first-come, first-served, except that a reading the user is waiting on takes precedence over a reading nothing is waiting on**. A user's own interactive listing is the first kind; an arrangement's walk is the second. **A walk in flight is not abandoned or rewound** — precedence acts at the next operation, not inside one — and no reading is starved, because a walk resumes as soon as the interactive reading it yielded to has been served. **T5's one-session lease is above this and unchanged**: where a command takes the primary lease, this channel closes after its current framed reply whatever it was serving, and this ordering applies again when it reopens.

   **A lease change is not a yield, and what it costs is stated rather than left to be met at Build. Added 2026-08-22, out of the third sanity sweep's verification round.** *A walk in flight is not abandoned or rewound* is true of yielding **inside** one channel, where the handles stay open. **A lease change closes the channel, and a handle does not survive its channel.** **Observed** on OpenSSH's own server subsystem, two processes driven directly over pipes, macOS 26.6.2, `OpenSSH_10.3p1`, 2026-08-22, with a control: a server read its own handle at offset 0 and returned `SSH_FXP_DATA`; a second server handed that same handle returned `SSH_FXP_STATUS` code **4, `Failure`**; and after the first channel was closed, a third server handed it returned the same. **The handle is a per-invocation value** — four bytes, an index into that server's own table. A negative would have looked like either of the last two returning data.

   **And this is observed on a rented machine, not only on the caller's end — corrected 2026-08-23; this paragraph named only the Mac and the rented half was already closed.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md) **Test 4**, 2026-08-22, posed it on a rental *"with both channels opened before anything was closed, so that closing one could not be confused with the connection going away"*: the handle read correctly on its own channel, was refused `Failure` on a second already-open channel of the same connection, was refused after the first channel was closed, and was refused on a freshly opened third channel. **The control is what makes it a handle result rather than a channel one** — *"the second channel then served `stat` on the same path and returned size 21. **So the channel was alive and it was the handle that was refused.**"* That input document names this clause as what the observation is behind. **So the rule below stands on a rental and not on an inference from the caller's own end.**

   **So an operation holding an open handle when the lease changes resumes by re-opening, and never by re-using.** Each such operation keeps, on the desktop side, what re-opening needs: `File Browser`'s prefix read keeps the path and its byte offset and re-opens at that offset; a walk keeps the directory path and the entries it has already returned, re-opens the directory and discards what it has. **What is genuinely lost is the server's own place in an enumeration**, so a re-opened walk re-reads that one directory from its start — the cost is one directory re-listed, not a walk rewound. **And what a re-opened enumeration cannot promise is what no re-opened enumeration can**: a directory whose contents changed between the two openings may yield an entry twice or not at all, which is the same weakness [7.4](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) already states for a resumed transfer, named here rather than discovered.

**Observed, on the caller's own end**, macOS 26.5, `OpenSSH_10.2p1, LibreSSL 3.3.6`, 2026-08-17: `/usr/libexec/sftp-server -Q requests` returns `open close read write lstat fstat setstat fsetstat opendir readdir remove mkdir rmdir realpath stat rename readlink symlink posix-rename statvfs fstatvfs hardlink fsync lsetstat limits expand-path copy-data home-directory users-groups-by-id`. Driven directly over a pipe, that server announced protocol version **3** and the extensions `posix-rename@openssh.com`, `statvfs@openssh.com`, `fstatvfs@openssh.com`, `hardlink@openssh.com`, `fsync@openssh.com`, `lsetstat@openssh.com`, `limits@openssh.com`, `expand-path@openssh.com`, `copy-data`, `home-directory` and `users-groups-by-id@openssh.com`, and reported its own limits as **max-packet 262,144, max-read 261,120, max-write 261,120, max-open-handles 1,048,571**. **This was observed on the caller's end and not on any rented machine**; [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 3 records that and what it costs.

**Before hand-over neither mechanism requires a Smoozy component on the machine, and none exists.** The subsystem is the machine's own file-transfer server, and the command channel carries only admissible commands naming programs the machine already carries. That is what makes the surface available before the package exists and after the package is destroyed, and it is [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server)'s *"Before the server package exists, no Smoozy component is installed on the machine"* read literally and kept: **the first Smoozy byte a machine ever executes is the package's own entry point, and only after [B10](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection)'s verification has passed on its staged bytes.** After hand-over the command channel's constant strings are that verified entry point and the admissible constant commands of the rule below; the subsystem mechanism needs no Smoozy component in either phase. **Nothing in this document reads the surface answering as evidence that Smoozy's installation is there** — that is [4.5](#45-a-reconnection-is-gated-on-the-installation-identity-and-not-on-reachability).

**What makes a command admissible, and it is the rule a builder checks each one against.** **It binds in both phases — corrected 2026-08-31; this was written as a pre-hand-over rule while the elevated work of two documents runs on the channel after hand-over too.** A command string contains only bytes of two classes, and a value with no admissible carriage does not ride the command channel in either phase.

1. **Fixed literals.** Bytes fixed at build time by the document that owns the step — the program's name, its options, its explicit output format, a constant unit, package or destination name. That a named program is present under the login on an arbitrary rental is the same unestablished ground every command-channel reading already stands on ([Section 12](#12-unresolved) items 3 and 11; [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 9); a program that is not there answers with its absence, which is a reportable result rather than a hole in the rule.
2. **Smoozy-synthesised tokens.** A path or name synthesised by the one document that owns it from an identity Smoozy issued ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#21-the-rules-that-bind-every-identity) §2.1 rule 7), drawn from lower-case letters, digits, hyphens and the path separator under an installation-owned prefix. [S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it)'s staging path is the exemplar, and this clause fixes that alphabet for every path this document synthesises. A token so drawn is syntax on no route.

**Every other value is carried off the command string, by exactly one of five routes** *(four until 2026-09-23, when the fifth was routed in from `Server Setup` E7)*.

- **The subsystem's own protocol fields.** *"This protocol represents file names as strings"* — `draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02`, read 2026-08-22 — length-delimited fields that no shell ever parses. This is why every file operation, attribute reading, free-space extension reading and transfer byte needs no command at all, in either phase.
- **The constant command's standard input, read as data.** Admissible only where the receiving program's own documentation says that input is data; the elevating command's password ([1.3](#13-what-the-surface-does-not-carry)) is the standing instance.
- **The planted-link carriage, for the one class with no other route: a machine-origin or user-origin path that a machine-side program must be pointed at before hand-over.** The path is written as the **target of a symbolic link** whose own name is a class-2 token, through the subsystem's symlink operation, so the value crosses as a protocol field and the constant command names the token, never the value. The operation is version 3's own — `SSH_FXP_SYMLINK`, `uint32 id, string linkpath, string targetpath` (`draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02` §6.10, read 2026-08-22), in [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)'s observed request set — **and OpenSSH's server takes the two strings in the reverse of the specification's order**, by its own `PROTOCOL` §4.1, read 2026-08-22: *"the order of the arguments to the SSH_FXP_SYMLINK method was inadvertently reversed … For correct operation, clients should send SSH_FXP_SYMLINK as follows: uint32 id, string targetpath, string linkpath."* **Two duties ride with the carriage.** The owning document cites the consuming program's own documented treatment of a link, because following is per-program and never assumed. And where the step's effect must land at the target — `Server Setup`'s hand-over of a chosen root is the case — the landing is confirmed by that document's own real-write check at the exact target path, never by the command's exit alone. The link is the desktop's own write on the authenticated connection, lives at its token path under a Smoozy-named directory in the login's own writable area — never on the mount it points at — and is removed when the step ends, as recorded staging under [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s discipline: an owed cleanup where the removal could not run, never a forgotten one.
- **Machine self-derivation, for the one class no carriage fits: a non-path value about the connection's own login that a machine-side program must consume as an argument, and that the invoking process's own credentials answer.** The command string stays constant — fixed literals embedding a double-quoted command substitution the login shell expands at execution — so the value originates on the machine, becomes an argument there, and never crosses from Smoozy at all. Three citations carry it, each the thing's own, all in the sources companion. The shell behind the string: *"All commands are run under the user's login shell as specified in the system password database"* (`sshd(8)`, LOGIN PROCESS, read 2026-08-22). The substitution: the shell replaces it *"with the standard output of the command, removing sequences of one or more <newline> characters at the end of the substitution"*, and inside double-quotes *"field splitting and pathname expansion shall not be performed on the results of the substitution"* (Shell Command Language §2.6.3, read 2026-08-22) — so the derived value is one argument and its bytes are data on every route, never re-parsed as syntax. The derivation: *"If no user operand is provided, the id utility shall write the user and group IDs and the corresponding user and group names of the invoking process to standard output"*, with `-u` the **effective** user ID and `-n` the name for it (POSIX `id`, read 2026-08-22) — the invoking process is the one `sshd` runs as the authenticated login, and no session underlies the value, which is exactly the ground `Rented-server runtime` K6's *self* form lacked. **The standing instance is K5/K6's lingering grant and read-back, and the route reaches no further than credentials the invoking process itself carries**: it never carries a value read off the machine earlier and echoed back — a package name, a listing result, a path — which stays inadmissible in a command string whatever its origin. That the login shell implements those semantics on an arbitrary rental is the same unestablished per-image ground every pre-hand-over command already stands on ([Section 12](#12-unresolved) items 3 and 11), and the failure is loud rather than silent — a mangled substitution names no login the machine holds, and K5 fails the placement before the commit. **The route adds no trust, and what the derived value is worth is K5/K6's to state**: it is the machine's own claim about itself, consumed on the machine, and never consumed by Smoozy as a value.

- **A package name the machine's own detection tool answers, for the one class the fourth carriage's bound excludes and no other carriage reaches: a name a machine-side program must consume as an argument, which is a choice the machine's own tool makes rather than a value anybody hands it. Added 2026-09-23, routed from [Server Setup](Server%20Setup.md#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password) E7 out of step 10c's fifth pass.** `Server Setup` R1 item 5's Ubuntu arm is the standing and only instance: the publication's own additional-components install names a driver branch, the branch is the distribution's detection route's choice, and that clause forbids the owning document naming a flavour. **The mechanism is the fourth carriage's exactly** — fixed literals embedding a double-quoted command substitution the login shell expands at execution, so the value originates on the machine, becomes one argument there, and never crosses from Smoozy — and the three citations behind it are the fourth's own: the login shell (`sshd(8)`, LOGIN PROCESS), the substitution's own semantics and its one-word quoting inside double quotes (Shell Command Language §2.6.3). **What is widened is the class of value and nothing else.** The fourth reaches no further than the credentials the invoking process carries; this one reaches a name the machine's own tool answers, and it is bounded three ways: **the tool asked is named in the constant string** and is not a value either; **the derived value is used only as an argument to a package operation, always inside double quotes, and is never a path, an option or a second command**; and **the owning document quotes the tool's own publication for what that tool prints**, because a derivation written from memory is what [Rule 2](../Groundwork/Rules.md) forbids. **What stays inadmissible is unchanged and is the thing this is most easily confused with**: a value Smoozy **reads off the machine and echoes back** into a command string — a package name, a listing result, a path — is still refused, and so is a value of user or provider origin, whatever its shape. The difference is whether Smoozy ever holds the value, and on this route it never does. **The route adds no trust**: the machine's own tool answering about the machine's own hardware is worth exactly what [A6](#3-the-authentication-boundary)'s standing answer says, and where it answers nothing the command names no package and the step fails loudly rather than installing something else.

**What the rule closes, and what it does not.** It closes the carriage: no pre-hand-over command interprets a value as syntax, whoever runs it — which is all the framing rule of item 1 ever existed to stop; the rule was a prohibition on interpolation, never a requirement for an installed file. It does not close item 9's ground — which programs answer on an arbitrary rental, and what their output is — and it adds no trust: **what stands behind every pre-hand-over command is [Section 3](#3-the-authentication-boundary)'s connection and the machine's own programs, worth exactly what the machine's honesty is worth, which is [A6](#3-the-authentication-boundary)'s standing answer and is not softened here.**

**One framed request after hand-over copies a file on the machine, and the entry point answers it itself. Added 2026-10-02, out of step 10c's map of Files' owed acts (its hole 2, first form), ruled by the build runner; no auditor of this document has seen it.** `File Browser` M7 copies by the subsystem's copy-data extension where it is announced and otherwise by *"the entry point's framed request after hand-over"*, and no document named that request. **It is *a file is copied on the machine***: it carries the source path and the staging path `File Browser` M7 synthesises, as frame fields and never on the command string; the entry point copies the source to the staging path, flushes it to disk and answers. **It is answered in the invocation that carries it and is never passed to the supervisor** — the one exception to [P1](#2-request-framing-and-versioning)'s passing — because it reads and writes no store the supervisor keeps (`Rented-server runtime` S1), and the supervisor as built serves its callers one after another, so a copy of many gigabytes there would hold every other request behind it. **Publication is the desktop's**, by `File Browser`'s own rename. It is in the declared request set ([1.2](#12-capability-negotiation-and-it-is-load-bearing-rather-than-a-courtesy) item 3) from the package that first carries it; a package that does not declare it has no such route.

**One framed request after hand-over reads the four values of several files, and the entry point answers it itself. Added 2026-10-03, out of step 10c's slow send (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/After the Vast fixes, the whole desk, four rulings and the slow send — what landed.md`, part 3), ruled by the build runner; no auditor of this document has seen it.** `Dependency Upload & Remap` Q5 route 1 reads four values off a machine copy — size, inode, modification time and change time — on the command channel, because revision 3's attributes carry neither the inode nor the change time. Until this request the one route was `stat -L` pointed at each path by the planted-link carriage: per file, a link planted and taken away (four subsystem requests) and one command, each through the per-server admission point ([T5](#62-concurrency-and-one-admission-point-rather-than-one-per-server)), one file after another — most of what a cache folder sent through the dependency check waited on. **The request is *the four values of files on the machine***. It carries one or more machine paths as frame fields, never on the command string. For each path the entry point reads the four values with the filesystem call that follows a link, as `stat -L` does, and answers them **in the units the command route reads — size in bytes, the inode number, and both times in whole seconds** — so a placement record written from either route compares against a reading by the other. A path with nothing there answers *nothing there*; a path it could not read answers *not read*, with the machine's own words beside it. **It is answered in the invocation that carries it and never passed to the supervisor**, as the copy above is: it reads no store the supervisor keeps (`Rented-server runtime` S1), and it is no worker and adds no kind. **Under [C6](#42-idempotent-submission) it is a pure reading.** **It carries as many paths as one frame holds**, and the caller puts the rest in its next ask. **Why it carries several, which is the point of it:** the entry point is itself reached through a planted link (item 1's correction of 2026-09-29), so one ask per file would cost exactly what the command route costs. The saving is one link and one command shared by many files; `Dependency Upload & Remap` Q5 says how its readings are gathered into one ask. It is in the declared request set ([1.2](#12-capability-negotiation-and-it-is-load-bearing-rather-than-a-courtesy) item 3) from the package that first carries it, and **where the installed package does not declare it, the four-value reading stays on the command route**, one file at a time. `Render Settings` Q14 item 2's reading of the `.blend`, one per page opening, is not moved. **Built, and its shape settled, 2026-11-04, out of a real folder of 480 files (`Audits/Build/Blender 5.3/The film's faults — what each one is.md`, fault 5), on the build runner's ruling; the paragraph above had stood unbuilt since 2026-10-03. No auditor has seen it.** The paths ride **one** frame field, each ended by a zero byte, which no path can hold. The answer is one field of text, one line for each path in the order asked: the four numbers, *nothing there*, or *not read* with the machine's own words, cut to 180 bytes. **One ask carries at most 4,000 paths and 512 KiB of them**, the caller's rule, so that the worst answer also fits the one mebibyte frame either end reads. **Publication and folder confirmation use this same request**, with grouping and missing-path handling owned by `Dependency Upload & Remap` Q5 route 1 and Q7 (2026-11-06). **No protocol version moves for it**, as none moved for the copy or the count above: P2's version names the request set, but this request is added by a package and not by a version, on this paragraph's own terms. **A package without it says so by name** (the framing's reserved field), its supervisor refusing what its adapter passed on, and that refusal is how the desktop learns the package does not carry it: as built, a release's declared request set is written empty, so there is nothing else to read. The one refused ask costs one command and claims nothing.

**One framed request after hand-over counts a folder's size, and the entry point answers it itself. Added 2026-10-30, a want under Rule 7 (the Creator's twenty-third note 2), entering at Specs `File Browser`; no auditor of this document has seen it.** **The request is *a folder's size is counted on the machine***. It carries one machine path as a frame field, never on the command string. The entry point walks everything below that path by `File Browser` L26's rules — links never followed, the system's in-memory pseudo filesystems not descended, an unreadable directory skipped — and answers **total bytes (apparent size), the count of files, the count of folders, and *incomplete* where anything was skipped**. A path with nothing there answers *nothing there*; a path that is not a directory answers *not a folder*; a top path it could not read answers *not read*, with the machine's own words beside it. **It is answered in the invocation that carries it and never passed to the supervisor**, as the two above are: it reads no store the supervisor keeps (`Rented-server runtime` S1), and a walk of minutes there would hold every other request behind it. **Under [C6](#42-idempotent-submission) it is a pure reading.** It reports nothing until the walk ends. It is in the declared request set ([1.2](#12-capability-negotiation-and-it-is-load-bearing-rather-than-a-courtesy) item 3) from the package that first carries it; where the installed package does not declare it, there is no route, and `File Browser` L28 does not offer the act.

### 1.2 Capability negotiation, and it is load-bearing rather than a courtesy

**Three things are negotiated and each is negotiated in its own way. None is inferred from a version number, and none is carried over from a previous connection.**

1. **The file-transfer subsystem's announced extension set.** The server states it in its opening reply, once per channel. It is read on the **first subsystem channel of a connection**, whichever kind it is, and recorded against the **connected period** — never against the server row, because the machine may have been replaced under the address ([4.5](#45-a-reconnection-is-gated-on-the-installation-identity-and-not-on-reachability)).

   **This set does vary between rentals, observed 2026-08-22, and that is what makes this negotiation load-bearing rather than a courtesy.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md) read it on three machines across three sellers: the two servers on OpenSSH 9.x announced **eleven** — `posix-rename@openssh.com`, `statvfs@openssh.com`, `fstatvfs@openssh.com`, `hardlink@openssh.com`, `fsync@openssh.com`, `lsetstat@openssh.com`, `limits@openssh.com`, `expand-path@openssh.com`, `copy-data`, `home-directory`, `users-groups-by-id@openssh.com` — and the server on **8.9p1** announced the **first eight**, missing `copy-data`, `home-directory` and `users-groups-by-id@openssh.com`. **No promise of this document's rests on the three that vary, and one consumer's does — corrected 2026-08-31 at the first audited reading of this document against [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md); this said *Smoozy leans on none of the three that vary*, which is a statement about the plan and not this document's to make.** [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) M7 takes `copy-data` as its preferred machine-side copy route and falls back to a copy through the command channel where it is not announced, so an 8.9p1 rental **degrades a copy** rather than breaking a transfer. Every extension this document itself leans on was present on all three. **The declared limits were byte-identical across all three despite the version gap.** **So the variance is in the tail and an older but entirely ordinary rental is a real case rather than a hypothetical** — which is exactly the situation the rule *a capability not announced is never attempted* exists for.
2. **The subsystem's declared operation limits** — the largest read, the largest write, the largest packet, and the number of handles — taken from the extension that reports them where the server announces it, and otherwise from a fixed conservative pair: **32,768 bytes for the largest read and 32,768 bytes for the largest write**. **That pair is the protocol's own recommendation and not a guarantee anyone makes** — corrected 2026-08-21, having been carried as Smoozy's own invention. The protocol version this document's [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) observed announced is **3**, and `draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02` — which states *"The version number of the protocol specified in this document is 3."* and which OpenSSH's own `PROTOCOL` file names as the standard its extensions deviate from — says in its Section 3 (read 2026-08-20 at `https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/id/draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02.txt`): *"All servers SHOULD support packets of at least 34000 bytes (where the packet size refers to the full length, including the header above). This should allow for reads and writes of at most 32768 bytes."* **That is the number, its origin and its reason in the specification's own words**, and it is also where `sftp(1)`'s *"a 32KB buffer"* default comes from — the standard client already puts that size on a server that announces nothing ([T7](#62-concurrency-and-one-admission-point-rather-than-one-per-server)), so a request of that size is one an ordinary client makes of every server this document can meet. **What stays true is the caution rather than the invention:** the draft says **SHOULD** and not **MUST**, so what an unannouncing server will actually accept is recommended by the specification and guaranteed by nobody; [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 3 labels that and says what breaks. **The branch has also never been reached** — the one rental this project has interrogated announced `limits@openssh.com` and gave real figures ([Fact 93](Facts.md)). **The write chunk size is chosen inside that bound, and [R6](#82-the-enforcement-point-is-smoozys-own-byte-loop-and-the-evidence-forced-it) bounds the rate-accounting interval in time as well as in bytes.**
3. **The command adapter's declared request set for the installed package version**, returned by package inspection ([Section 5](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection)). This is the only one of the three that exists solely after hand-over.

**Why the first is load-bearing, and it is observed rather than argued.** The base protocol's own rename operation **refuses to replace an existing target**: driven against OpenSSH's own server subsystem over a pipe on the Creator's Mac, macOS 26.5, `OpenSSH_10.2p1`, on 2026-08-17, `SSH_FXP_RENAME` of a staging path over an existing final path returned status **4, `Failure`**, while `posix-rename@openssh.com` over the same existing target returned status **0, `Success`**, the published file held only the new content, its inode had changed, and the staging name was gone. **So publication over an existing name is an extension rather than a protocol operation**, and [7.5](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step) states what is done where the extension is not announced.

**A capability that is not announced is never attempted and never guessed at.** Where a request has no negotiated route, the caller reports it as unavailable against that connection — which is a statement about the connection, not a failure of the work — and the module that wanted it decides. **A silent fallback to a route that cannot keep [Section 7](#7-staging-integrity-resume-and-publication--one-contract-in-both-directions)'s contract is forbidden outright**; [7.5](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s degradation is declared rather than improvised.

### 1.3 What the surface does not carry

**No secret is ever a command argument or an environment value**, which is [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#13-logging-redaction) §13 rules 1 and 2 and is not softened here: a second ordinary login on a rental sees another login's process *"with its owner and command line"* (Fact 60). The one secret that crosses and stays on a machine is the Telegram bot token, placed by `Server Setup` as material rather than as an argument ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#10-what-smoozy-leaves-on-a-rented-machine) §10). The elevated-access password crosses whenever a step needs it, on the elevating command's standard input, and is never kept on the machine ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#12-the-domains-and-the-complete-crossing-list) §1.2).

**No tool's own output is mined for a cause.** [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#13-logging-redaction) §13 rule 5 states it and Fact 37 is the reason — *no OpenSSH manual documents a single one of the diagnostic strings the tools emit*. Two consequences bind this document. A transport failure and a remote command's own failure both arrive at exit status **255** and *"it is the accompanying text that separates them, not the number"* (Fact 7), so **no request result is classified on an exit status alone** where 255 is one of the possibilities. And the three listing failures — may-not-read, gone, did-not-answer — are distinguishable *"only by reading the message, never the exit status"* on the command channel, so **a request that needs those three told apart is carried on the file-transfer subsystem**, whose status replies carry a code of their own, rather than on the command channel. **The subsystem separates the stat case too, corrected 2026-08-20, and this paragraph used to say it did not.** The protocol defines two different status codes and the server returns them — the SSH file-transfer protocol draft version 3, the version [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) negotiates, read 2026-08-20: `SSH_FX_NO_SUCH_FILE 2`, *"returned when a reference is made to a file which should exist but doesn't"*, and `SSH_FX_PERMISSION_DENIED 3`, *"returned when the authenticated user does not have sufficient permissions to perform the operation."* Run down a real subsystem on 2026-08-20 with a control on each side: an attribute request on a file that exists under an unreadable parent returned **status 3**; the same request on a path that does not exist returned **status 2**; the same request on a readable file returned attributes. Three inputs, three answers. **Label: the two status codes and their meanings are documented, quoted above from the draft; that a real server returns them at an attribute request is observed. Both labels recorded 2026-08-31, at the first audited reading of this document against [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md), which had carried this half unlabelled.** **What the observation is, and it is stronger than `File Browser` P1 still says it is:** the run was re-performed on **2026-08-23** with the control on each side and the same three answers, on the Creator's Mac — macOS 26.6.2, build 25G83, `OpenSSH_10.3p1`, driving the machine's own `/usr/libexec/sftp-server` directly over pipes — and **a negative would have looked like the first two returning the same status as each other, which is what the transfer client program does, or either returning attributes**. The machine, the version and the negative are all in this document's [Sources](Sources/Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.sources.md) companion, and P1's *no observation document holds it, and neither the machine, the software version nor a statement of what a negative would have looked like is written down anywhere* was true on 2026-08-20 and has not been true since 2026-08-23. **What is genuinely not established is narrower and is [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 24's**: no rented machine's own file-transfer server has been posed at the stat case, and this is the caller's own end. **What the old sentence rested on is Fact 7's other half** — *"where it happens at **stat**, because the login cannot traverse a parent, it says `not found`, **byte-identical to a path that never existed**"* — which is the transfer **client program** rendering both cases the same way because it expands the path before it asks, and that collapse was reproduced today on the same rig. **Fact 7's own next line already held the right half**: on the file-transfer route the three cases carry *"distinct protocol statuses"*, with a control either side. **So what is supplied to `File Browser` is a separation rather than a limit**: at readdir and at stat alike, the subsystem tells refusal from absence in one request, and nothing downstream need tell a user the two cannot be told apart. **The command channel is a different matter and is not this** — there a test on the path separates nothing by exit status, and only a walk of the parent components does ([Rendering](Rendering.md) A7). What `File Browser` does with a refusal it has identified, including whether elevated access is offered, is its own.

---

## 2. Request framing and versioning

**Constraint:** the scope above, and [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#8-the-supervisors-machine-local-endpoint) §8 requirement 3 — *"Every request is still framed, versioned and correlated as `Server protocol and transfers` defines. The endpoint is not a trusted channel that skips validation; it is a channel whose peer is known."*

**P1. One adapter invocation frames exactly one request and returns exactly one correlated result.** The command adapter is short-lived, authenticates by the SSH connection, passes the framed request to the supervisor over the machine-local endpoint, and returns the result ([The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server)). *(One request is answered by the adapter itself and never reaches the supervisor: the on-machine copy of [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one), added 2026-10-02. It controls no queue, job or worker, which is what The shape's sentence is about.)* *(Another, added 2026-10-03: the four-value reading of [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one). It too controls no queue, job or worker, and its several paths are one request.)* **No invocation carries two requests**, because a partly-delivered pair has no answer either side could give — which is [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#91-the-partition-unit) §9.1's partition unit applied to the wire: one request is one unit.

**The framing unit is one connection to the machine-local endpoint carrying one framed request and one correlated result, and an adapter invocation is one caller of it rather than the only one. Added 2026-09-04, out of a Build sitting; no auditor of this document has seen it.** Two of the endpoint's callers are on the machine and neither is an adapter invocation: [`Rented-server runtime`](Rented-server%20runtime.md#24-the-health-exchange-and-what-a-failure-restores) G12's health exchange, which is *"one request over the machine-local endpoint to the supervisor the commit has just activated"* and says in terms that it **is framed like any other request**; and that document's [W12](Rented-server%20runtime.md#52-per-operation-workers) worker report, which its M6 gives **one channel to the supervisor** and which is re-attempted against the endpoint at Z2's recorded path. [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#8-the-supervisors-machine-local-endpoint) §8 requirement 3 already binds every request that reaches the endpoint to this document's framing, whoever sent it, and the scope line above already puts *request framing over the machine-local endpoint* here. **What binds each caller is the same and nothing is added for the machine-side ones:** one request per connection, no connection carrying two, the result correlated by [C1](#41-correlation-is-by-operation-identity-and-the-identity-is-the-callers)'s identity, and the fields bound by P5 below. **The clause read as though the adapter were the only framer**, which left a builder of either machine-side caller with no stated unit at all.

**P2. Two versions travel and they are not one field.** The **protocol version** names the request framing, the request set, the digest function ([S23](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)) and the one rate unit every budget, enforcement and reading in this document is carried in ([R11](#83-a-cap-reaches-transfers-already-running-which-is-row-306)). The **package version** names the installed material. A request carries the protocol version the caller speaks; the result carries the version **the answering side** speaks and the package version it was built from. **Corrected 2026-09-04, out of a Build sitting; no auditor of this document has seen it** — this read *the version the adapter speaks*, and the answerer of a request a worker or the package transaction framed is the supervisor rather than an adapter. `Rented-server runtime` G12's own health answer is the standing instance: it carries **the package version the supervisor was built from**.

**P3. Compatibility is current plus immediately previous, and a package commit may not strand accepted work.** Every desktop and server manifest declares its current protocol version and, where one exists, the single immediately previous supported protocol version; **no wider or skipped-version interval is accepted**. Negotiation chooses the current version where equal, otherwise that one immediately previous version where it is the intersection; an empty intersection is **incompatible**, never a guessed downgrade. A server package remains able to inspect, stop, release and collect every operation it accepted under either declared version until those operations have ended and their results have been acknowledged. The package-phase record therefore keeps the immediately previous adapter runnable beside the candidate adapter while any live or held operation still needs it. A commit that would remove the last route to such work remains deferred; a later desktop uses the compatible previous adapter to inspect that work, offer or perform the already-defined stop/release action, and collect its result. **Indefinite live or held work delays package activation, not control of the work and not the desktop's ability to use the server.** Once no accepted operation depends on the previous version, the candidate pointer is committed and the previous adapter can be retired under the package-phase record.

**P3a. A caller that meets a protocol interval with no overlap treats that adapter as unavailable and never guesses at a shape.** It does not submit a new operation and does not parse a result it cannot frame. It first uses package inspection to locate any retained adapter whose interval overlaps and uses that adapter only for the inspection, stop, release and collection routes P3 preserves. Where there is no compatible retained adapter and no live or held work, the ordinary supervisor-unavailable path of [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) applies: the caller reports the supervisor as unavailable **without marking the server connection down**, inspects the package, and the automatic repair `Desktop app and delivery` owns invokes [Section 5](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection). Where live or held work exists but no retained compatible adapter can control it, repair fails closed and reports the exact work identity; it does not replace the only remaining route to that work.

**P3b. An interval with no overlap is reported as *incompatible*, with its direction, and never as the supervisor not answering. Added 2026-09-28, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build. Label: Creator-stated (2026-09-28), carried from their rule for the application's own update. No auditor of this document has seen it.** P3a's *reports the supervisor as unavailable* was a statement about a program that is running and answering, and `Desktop app and delivery` V1 would present it as *Smoozy isn't responding*, which is false. **So the caller reports a condition of its own — *incompatible* — and never raises V1's.** Its direction comes from B4's inspection and nothing else: **the active package's release sequence against the application's own release sequence** (B8d). Below it is *too old*; above it is *too new*. Package inspection composes the whole of that reading over the pre-package surface, so it needs nothing from the program it describes. **What is done about each is `Desktop app and delivery` V11 to V16's**, and P3a's two routes are unchanged underneath: *too old* with no live or held work is replaced by V11's update, which is the same request under the same commit and rollback rules as the repair; *too old* with live or held work and no retained compatible adapter defers, reports that its work cannot be controlled from the desktop, and replaces nothing; *too new* is never replaced from this desktop at all (V14). **Whether live or held work exists is read the way G21 reads it** — from the records, through the surface — which is what `State, identity and recovery` §1.3 rule 7 keeps possible on a program the desktop cannot talk to.

**P4. The repair route is never versioned by the thing it repairs.** The bootstrap-and-repair request rides on the pre-package surface and is framed by this document rather than by the installed adapter, so a package too old to be spoken to can still be replaced. **This is the structural reason the request is independent of the supervisor's endpoint** rather than a convenience.

**P5. Every request field is either required by this protocol version or ignored.** An adapter that meets a field it does not know ignores it rather than refusing the request, and a caller that meets a result field it does not know ignores it rather than refusing the result. **The one field kind that is not merely ignored but forbidden is a field naming the caller's own identity** — [Section 3](#3-the-authentication-boundary) — because ignoring it and checking it are the same thing for security and only one of them is safe to write down.

**P6. Nothing about a request's framing is a secret.** The machine-local endpoint's path is not a credential and is not treated as one ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#8-the-supervisors-machine-local-endpoint) §8), and neither is a request's shape.

**P7. A path is bytes on the machine side and the receiving system's own path unit on the desktop side, and it is text nowhere. Ruled 2026-09-06 under the Creator's standing delegation of 2026-08-31; no auditor of this document has seen it.** This is the whole seam settled once. It binds this document's wire, this document's record and the hand-over at either end, and the two documents that meet it — [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) L1b and [`Desktop app and delivery`](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md) F10b — cite it rather than restate it. Five parts.

1. **On the wire a path is bytes, because the protocol says that and says nothing more.** The version [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) negotiates is revision 3, and the subsystem's own author says it will never be another: *"Note that OpenSSH's sftp and sftp-server implement revision 3 of the SSH filexfer protocol"* and *"Newer versions of the draft will not be supported, though some features are individually implemented as extensions described below."* (OpenSSH, `PROTOCOL`, release 8.9p1, read 2026-09-06 at `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openssh/openssh-portable/V_8_9_P1/PROTOCOL`.) That revision's whole statement about a name is *"This protocol represents file names as strings. File names are assumed to use the slash ('/') character as a directory separator."* — no encoding, no character set, nothing (§6.2, *SSH File Transfer Protocol*, draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02, October 2001, read 2026-09-06 at `https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02.txt`). **The silence is deliberate rather than an omission**: the same draft tags the one field it means as text, `string error message (ISO-10646 UTF-8 [RFC-2279])`, and tags no path. And a `string` is *"Arbitrary length binary string. Strings are allowed to contain arbitrary binary data, including null characters and 8-bit characters."* (RFC 4251 §5, read 2026-09-06 at `https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc4251.txt`.) **Documented.**

2. **On the rented machine the bytes are what the filesystem holds.** POSIX's own definition of a filename is *"A sequence of bytes consisting of 1 to {NAME\_MAX} bytes used to name a file. The bytes composing the name shall not contain the &lt;NUL&gt; or &lt;slash&gt; characters."*, and the same definition goes on to say a filename followed by a null *"forms a string (but not necessarily a character string)"* — **the standard says outright that a name need not be text** (§3.170, The Open Group Base Specifications Issue 7, 2018 edition, IEEE Std 1003.1-2017, read 2026-09-06 at `https://pubs.opengroup.org/onlinepubs/9699919799/basedefs/V1_chap03.html`), and ext4's own kernel documentation counts the same unit — *"Since file names cannot be longer than 255 bytes"* — over a `char name[EXT4_NAME_LEN]` field (read 2026-09-06 at `https://docs.kernel.org/filesystems/ext4/directory.html`). **Documented**, and Fact 24's own observation says the same from the other side: *"Linux permits any byte but `/` and NUL"*.

3. **On the user's own computer the unit is the system's own and it is not the same unit on all three, which is why *bytes everywhere* would be false on one of them.** Apple's own reference holds a directory entry's name in a `uint8_t name[]` field and says *"The name, represented as a null-terminated UTF-8 string."* (*Apple File System Reference*, `j_drec_key_t` and `j_drec_hashed_key_t`, page dated 2020-06-22, read 2026-09-06 at `https://developer.apple.com/support/downloads/Apple-File-System-Reference.pdf`) — which is why Fact 24's measured `EILSEQ` on invalid UTF-8 is the system's rule and not a library's. Microsoft's is a different unit: *"NTFS stores file names in Unicode."* (*Character Sets Used in File Names*, page dated 2018-05-31, updated 2025-03-11, read 2026-09-06 at `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/intl/character-sets-used-in-file-names`), and *"On newer file systems, such as NTFS, exFAT, UDFS, and FAT32, Windows stores the long file names on disk in Unicode, which means that the original long file name is always preserved."* (*Naming Files, Paths, and Namespaces*, page dated 2024-08-28, updated 2025-04-11, read 2026-09-06 at `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/fileio/naming-a-file`). **Documented.** **So a desktop-side path is carried in the unit the system it runs on names files in and in no unit chosen here** — on macOS and Linux the path's own bytes, on Windows its UTF-16 code units — and where one is stored durably it is stored in that same unit, which is safe because the local record is one installation's and is never read on another computer ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) §1.1).

4. **The one place one becomes the other is the name check, and it is a judgement rather than a conversion.** [`Desktop app and delivery`](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#72-the-name-check-before-the-transfer) C3 already runs it against the receiving system and the receiving volume **before any byte moves**, and C4's four outcomes are already the answer. Machine-side bytes become a local path only where that check answered *writable as given*; otherwise it is one of C4's three refusals and nothing is converted, nothing is substituted and no byte moves. **In the other direction there is no conversion at all** — [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) N2 already puts the source name's own bytes on the wire and forbids anything rewriting, normalising or re-encoding it in flight.

5. **Text appears once and it is display.** A decode for showing may substitute for an undecodable byte, and what it produces is never compared, never stored, never put on the wire and never used to name anything — which is [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) L1a and X1 stated for this surface.

**What this binds in this document, exactly, and one thing that looked exempt and is not.** **Every path [S9](#73-the-progress-record-is-on-the-desktop-side-in-both-directions)'s record holds is on this rule, and each is held and stored in the unit *its own end* names** — so the same field is bytes on an upload and the desktop system's own unit on a download, the direction deciding it. The **final path** is the plain case. **S9's staging path is on it too, and a builder must not read [S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it) as exempting it.** S4 synthesises the staging **name** from the transfer identity and the attempt number and *"never from the file's own name"*, so that one component can carry nothing hostile — but [S1](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it) puts the whole thing *"on the same filesystem as the final name, in a Smoozy-named directory beside it"*, and beside the final name is a folder the user chose on a download and an arbitrary folder on the machine on an upload. **So the last component of a staging path is safe and its leading components are exactly as unsafe as the final path's**, and both fields are held in the same unit. Said here because *the staging path is synthesised* reads like an exemption and is not one.

**Why not text across the seam, said here so it is not re-argued.** Text is not the neutral choice it looks like. It contradicts three clauses written against watched failures — [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) L1a, N2 and X1 — and it costs exactly what [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 12 already records: a name this surface can move and a listing cannot read. A decode that substitutes hands every later comparison a name that is not the name, which is [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md)'s truthfulness promise broken quietly; a decode that refuses turns one bad entry into a failed listing, which is the raise already observed. **And it is not one decision but one taken again at every site that touches a path**, which is how the two spellings came to stand side by side in one file — `Desktop app and delivery`'s own record schema holds a transfer's paths as text and a neighbouring module's paths as bytes, with a comment on the second saying *"Every path is `BLOB` and never `TEXT`, and that is load-bearing rather than tidy."*

**What it costs the user, stated rather than assumed.** Almost nothing changes for them today, and this is a maintainability ruling rather than a user-facing one. **The one thing that does change is on the rented machine**: a file or folder whose name is not valid text can be listed, selected, uploaded into and fetched out of, where before it could not be reached at all. On a Mac such a name still cannot be written locally, and C3 says so before a byte moves — what changes is that the refusal names the true reason, *the name cannot be written on your computer*, rather than *the name cannot be sent*.

---

## 3. The authentication boundary

**Constraint:** [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#12-the-domains-and-the-complete-crossing-list) §1.2 — *"The SSH connection is the authentication boundary for everything: the pre-package surface, the versioned command protocol, the bootstrap-and-repair request and the transfer channels all inherit it, and `Server protocol and transfers` owns their framing."* And [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#8-the-supervisors-machine-local-endpoint) §8, which owns the local access boundary at the endpoint.

**A1. Every request on every one of the carriage routes is authenticated by the SSH connection and by nothing of its own.** There is no second credential, no per-request token, no bearer value and no signature. A request that arrived on the connection is authenticated; a request that did not arrive on it does not exist.

**A2. No request field names its caller, and any such field is ignored rather than checked.** That is [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#8-the-supervisors-machine-local-endpoint) §8 requirement 2 stated from the request side. The supervisor establishes the caller's local identity from the connection itself. **The mechanism is answered:** peer credentials on the endpoint returned `pid=1628 uid=1000 gid=1000` with `uid 1000` resolving to the caller's own login, unchanged under a container's reduced capability set, and a caller attempting to attach a `uid 0` claim was **refused outright** with `errno 1 Operation not permitted` while attaching nothing yielded the same truth, on both a container and a virtual machine — *"the endpoint cannot be lied to about who is calling"* ([The host key](The%20host%20key%20—%20obtained%20without%20authenticating,%20and%20whose%20it%20turns%20out%20to%20be.md), Fact 84). `Rented-server runtime` implements it.

**A3. A machine’s results are observations, never authority to alter desktop security.** A result carrying a permission or capability grant remains an observation; SSH and the machine-local endpoint retain their own authentication boundaries.

**A4. Nothing here retries an authentication.** [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#54-retry-and-three-things-that-are-not-one-another) §5.4 and [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#4-reaching-a-secret-and-giving-it-to-the-thing-that-needs-it) §4 state why, on observed evidence that repeated attempts corrupt the diagnosis. A request that failed to authenticate is reported as that and is not sent again on the same credential.

**A5. Host trust is `Security and licence` §7's and is cited rather than restated**, with one rule this document adds because it is a rule about correlation rather than about trust: **no request, no correlation and no resumption in this document is keyed on a machine's remembered identity.** The evidence is in *What binds this* above — a container's host key outlived the rental it belonged to, byte-identically, across a genuine replacement. The offered key is obtainable and comparable **before** any connection that would present Smoozy's own credential. Observed (Fact 82), in the observation's own words — the scan *"exited 0, wrote nothing to stderr"* and returned every key type the machine offers, and *"No identity file was supplied and none was asked for"*; and *"Two independent scans, minutes apart, returned byte-identical key material."* **The quotation is restored to the source's words 2026-08-23**, this having carried a one-line condensation of all three stated as a quotation of the fact; nothing it said went beyond what was observed, and `Servers` [Q4](Servers.md#101-host-trust-comes-first-and-the-scan-decides-row-36) carried the identical condensation and was repaired the same way. So the trust decision happens before a connection exists to correlate anything on. Everything below runs on a connection that decision has already permitted.

**A6. An authenticated connection is not evidence about the machine at the other end of it.** [4.5](#45-a-reconnection-is-gated-on-the-installation-identity-and-not-on-reachability) is the whole of what this document does about that.

---

## 4. Correlation, idempotent submission and atomic acknowledgement

**Constraint:** [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) §12's four rules to this document, quoted in that document and applied here rather than restated as a second set of rules. And [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) — *"Each command has an operation identity. Each caller waiting for a state transition has its own deadline and reports expiry without changing the operation's durable state. There is no shared assumption that a successful signal means the worker has already ended."*

### 4.1 Correlation is by operation identity, and the identity is the caller's

**C1. Every request carries an operation identity, chosen by the initiating side before the request is sent**, per [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#22-the-register-of-identities) §2.2. **It is the idempotency key and there is not a second value beside it.** It is opaque, never parsed, never derived from a path, a label, a host key, a process identifier or anything read off a machine — [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#21-the-rules-that-bind-every-identity) §2.1 entire binds it, and rule 7 is why the only path this document synthesises from an identity is the staging path it owns ([7.2](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it)).

**C2. A result is matched to a request by that identity and never by arrival order, never by channel, and never by the invocation that carried it.** That is what makes correlation survive a connection `Servers` replaced: *"the identity is the caller's and the record is the machine's; neither belongs to a connection"* ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) §12 rule 3).

**C3.
