The caller writes its pending-command record durably before the request leaves**, in the local record ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#22-the-register-of-identities) §2.2). A request that has been sent and has no local record is a request whose answer cannot be found, and no ordering below permits one.

### 4.2 Idempotent submission

**C4. The machine records the operation identity durably before acting on it** — in an operation record where the request starts a worker, in a command record where it does not ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#51-the-durable-operation-record) §5.1). **A request repeating an identity already recorded never acts again.**

**C5. So re-sending is the ordinary way a lost answer is resolved, and it is not a retry.** It carries the **same** operation identity, it does not touch the attempt count, and it is not a fresh attempt. A fresh attempt is a new operation with a new identity, and where it is a render a new job with a new folder — `Rendering`'s ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#54-retry-and-three-things-that-are-not-one-another) §5.4).

**C6. Every request this document defines is idempotent under C4 or is a pure reading.** A builder that finds a request that is neither has found a fault in this section. Concretely: a submission, a stop, a deletion, a transfer arrangement and the bootstrap-and-repair request are all recorded-before-acted; a listing, a file-attribute read, a free-space reading, a hardware reading, a digest over a byte range and package inspection are readings and carry an identity for correlation and reporting rather than for idempotency. **One request is of a third class: *a file is copied on the machine* ([1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)) is a write into a staging path only, made safe to repeat by the exclusive create of that path, and is neither recorded first nor a reading.** Added 2026-10-02, ruled by the build runner; no auditor of this document has seen it. **A job's kept crash files are one more pure reading** — `Reporting a problem` G5a's: the files the render worker kept for that job, each whole and named by its attempt, or the answer that there are none; it registers nothing and changes nothing. Added 2026-10-24 by step 11's plan pass; no auditor of this document has seen it.

### 4.3 Atomic acknowledgement, and what an acknowledgement is not

**C7. An acknowledgement says the request is recorded. It never says the work is done.** This is the whole of the term, and conflating the two is what Row 291 exists to prevent ([10.1](#101-the-rule-put-as-a-requirement-on-every-request-this-document-defines)).

**C8. Acknowledgement is atomic in the sense that there is no third state.** Because C4 puts the durable record before the act, the machine's answer to *what became of this identity* is one of exactly three things, and the set is closed:

- **A recorded outcome** — the operation ended, and the outcome is returned.
- **Still in hand** — the identity is recorded and has no outcome yet. It is returned as an answer, carrying the latest reconciliation disposition and the attempt count, and it is what a repeated request gets for an operation that is running, was resumed, or is on its second attempt ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) §12 rule 2). ***Still in hand* is an answer, not a silence.**
- **No record of that identity** — the request never arrived, or arrived and was refused before it was recorded. The work did not happen.

**One command's record does not outlive its close, and for it this third answer is narrower. Added 2026-09-19 by the build stage, from step 10c piece 5's finding.** The desktop's cursor move keeps C4 — recorded before it acts — but its command record ends when it closes (`Rented-server runtime` R1), because it acts on no subject whose record could end it. So an ask for its identity after the close answers *no record of that identity* for a move that happened. **R1 says why nothing turns on that**, and the set stays closed. *No auditor has seen this text.*

**Which of the three a machine has actually returned, said so the set is not read as three observations. Added 2026-08-23.** Fact 8 cut the connection mid-submission **312 times** with the operation identity chosen before the request was sent: every trial cut at 0.000–0.002 s answered **no record of that identity** and every trial from 0.004 s upward answered **a recorded outcome**, while ***still in hand* was never once returned** — *"the window in which a submission is accepted but has no recorded outcome is narrower than a shell can aim at."* **So the first and third answers are observed and the middle one is a requirement this document places on the machine**, and C4's record-before-act is what makes it exist at all. **The set is still closed** — the requirement is what closes it — and a machine returning anything else in that window is a fault in `Rented-server runtime` rather than a fourth state. **What the narrow window costs is only that the middle branch is untested**, which is [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 10's ground; what C9 needs is that an identity can be asked for, and that is what the 312 trials established.

**C9. A lost acknowledgement is resolved by asking the machine for the identity, never by re-issuing the work under a fresh one** ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) §12 rule 4). That is what makes Fact 8's question answerable, and it is why a job identity is issued before the submission is sent.

**C10. An acknowledgement lost in the pipe is indistinguishable from a request that never arrived, and nothing on the wire separates them.** Fact 72 is the reason: damage in the pipe kills the connection and *"the failure is a dropped connection, not a 'file is corrupt' message"*, with the machine's own log saying nothing. **So the separation is never attempted at the transport layer**; it is C8's three answers on the next connection, and no other route to it exists in this document.

### 4.4 Re-establishing after a connection Servers replaced

**C11. A replaced connection replaces the channels and nothing else.** The connected period, the reconnect policy and when a connection is made or replaced are `Servers`'. What this document does on a connection that has replaced another, in this order:

1. **Take [4.5](#45-a-reconnection-is-gated-on-the-installation-identity-and-not-on-reachability)'s gate.** Nothing below runs before it.
2. **Negotiate capabilities afresh** ([1.2](#12-capability-negotiation-and-it-is-load-bearing-rather-than-a-courtesy)). Nothing negotiated on the previous connection is carried over.
3. **Resolve every outstanding pending-command record for this server** by asking the machine for its identity under C8. This is `Rented-server runtime`'s record and needs the supervisor.
4. **Re-establish transfer channels for every transfer whose record says *interrupted* *and* which was interrupted within the current application run** ([7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)) **and, for an upload or a manual download, whose request is still open** — a request whose server's period ended is not (`File Browser` U7, `Download` M13; added 2026-09-28, outside any round, no auditor of this document has seen it) — and resume each under [7.4](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified). Nothing is resumed that the record does not name. **The gate is the ending itself and not a comparison against one other ending — corrected 2026-08-31; this said *interrupted rather than stopped*, which resumed every ending that was not *stopped*.** *Published*, *stopped*, *failed* and *stalled* are all refused here, because *interrupted* is the only ending whose work may be carried on ([S26b](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)); a resume over a *failed* record would resume over material S24 removed and re-send the whole file from zero under [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 1 with nothing said. **A record written by an earlier application run is not resumed here.** Its staged material is kept and the user is told it is there, which is Row 197; whether that arrangement is taken up again is `File Browser`'s and `Download`'s under C13, and the record carries which run wrote it so the two cases are separable.

**C12. Steps 3 and 4 have different requirements, and the difference is deliberate.** Step 3 needs the supervisor, because the record it asks about is the supervisor's. **Step 4 does not**, because a transfer's staged material and its staging path are Smoozy's own and the pre-package surface performs the same contract before hand-over as the transfer-support worker does after it ([The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server)). So a supervisor that does not answer while SSH is live stops command correlation and **does not stop transfers**, which is the shape's own statement — *"Browsing, file operations, readings, and transfers remain available; the pre-package surface carries transfers through publication and cleanup under the same contract while the supervisor is unavailable."*

**C13. Nothing here starts work a user did not ask for on a new connection.** A resumed transfer under step 4 carries on work the user started and a drop interrupted, which is what Rows 199 and 209 promise. **A transfer that ended with the application run does not restart itself on a later connection** — the specs draw that line and this document does not cross it; `File Browser` and `Download` own which of their arrangements survive a run.

### 4.5 A reconnection is gated on the installation identity and not on reachability

**Constraint:** the recycle observation in *What binds this*, and Fact 83's negative answer. *"A Smoozy that reconnects and finds its address answering has no signal that its whole installation is gone. It must check for its own state, not for reachability."*

**C14. Before any correlation is resumed, any staged material is trusted, or any remembered machine-derived value is used, the installation identity of [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#23-the-machines-installation-identity) §2.3 is read and its branch is taken.** That document owns the four outcomes and this one does not restate them. What this document does with each:

- **Present and equal, confirmed.** Correlation resumes, staged material is Smoozy's, transfers resume.
- **Absent**, or **present and different.** Every pending-command record, every transfer record and every staged partial held against this server row is **disqualified**: not resumed, not asked about, not published, and not read as a partial of anything. Staged material at a path this document remembers is not Smoozy's material, whatever its size — and on a genuine recycle it is gone anyway, observed *"down to the `cron` package"*. `Server Setup` owns what the user is offered.
- **Present, and the supervisor did not answer.** **Nothing is discarded.** Command correlation does not resume, because its records are the supervisor's. **Transfers do resume**, per C12, because the identity being present is what says the material is Smoozy's and the contract does not need the supervisor either before hand-over or during a supervisor outage.

**C15. The identity is read on the pre-package surface, from Smoozy's own state area, and never against a path a provider also writes.** The recycle observation is exact about why: the boot hook *"was not deleted but replaced"* by a provider default, so **presence is not a check**. [Section 5](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) applies the same rule to package inspection.

### 4.6 Per-waiter deadlines

**C16. A deadline belongs to the waiter and never to the request or the operation.** It is volatile — process memory for the life of the application run ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) §1.1) — and two waiters on one operation have two deadlines, either of which may expire while the other does not.

**C17. A waiter's expiry is reported and changes no durable state.** It is a statement about the waiter, never about the operation: it does not write an outcome, does not write a desired state, does not cancel, and does not count as an attempt. That is [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) and [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#53-the-deliberate-stop) §5.3 step 5, on the observed reason — a stop took effect on the target's next cycle, about 2.3 seconds against a three-second cycle, and *"A check made two seconds after sending the signal reported the program still running and would have been wrong"* ([Batch A](Batch%20A%20—%20the%20shape%20throwaway.md), Fact 2).

**What C16 and C17 bind is a waiter on an operation, and not the byte loop's own channel deadline.** A transfer channel's inactivity deadline ([C19](#46-per-waiter-deadlines)) is not a per-waiter deadline in this sense and its expiry does write durable state: **it ends the channel and the record says *stalled*** ([S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)). The two are different because the subject is different. Fact 2's caution is about being wrong concerning **the machine** — a target that had not yet reached its next cycle. Closing a channel Smoozy itself owns is a fact about what Smoozy did, and stopping a transfer is this document's precisely because the byte loop is ([C22](#47-cancellation-and-there-are-two-of-them)). A channel Smoozy never tore down and never recorded would leave [S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s *stalled* ending with no member at all, and [G7](#103-a-slow-thing-and-a-stopped-one)'s distinguishable stall with nothing built on it.

**C17a. An unanswered command-path request is evidence about the supervisor only where the transport that carried it is shown to have been alive across the silence, and the transport's own next report is what says so. Added 2026-08-29, out of the scenario replay of *The connection drops while rendering*: in the 45 to 60 seconds before `Servers` [K3](Servers.md#21-the-break-reporting-mechanism) declares a break of the user's own line, a request is ordinarily in flight, and nothing said whether its silence raised the supervisor-unavailable condition — C17 and `Queue & History` [A3](Queue%20&%20History.md#13-reading-a-machine-and-adoption) answered in opposite directions, and a builder was left the race.** The condition [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#83-the-condition-identity-and-who-supplies-each-observed-value) §8.3's table names is *the supervisor unavailable **while SSH is live***; its observed value is whether the supervisor answered a request on the command path and nothing else (`Desktop app and delivery` [V1](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#12-the-automatic-supervisor-unavailable-repair-trigger), `Rented-server runtime` V4); and a line dying under a request leaves that question **uncarried rather than answered no**. Read the other way, one expiry would raise a machine verdict that stood for the user's whole outage — withdrawn only on *the supervisor answering* (§8.3's table), with no request composed while the primary connection is not live (`Desktop app and delivery` [P10d](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#33-ui-event-delivery)) — beside the connection condition's own honest statement, and `Preparation/Specs/Whole app.md` forbids that in terms: *"A lost connection is a fact about the connection, never a verdict on the machine."*

- **The waiter's expiry raises nothing, which is C17 standing whole.** The expiry is a statement about the waiter; the observation it leaves — this request went unanswered — is held volatile against the transport's next report; and the pending-command record stands untouched, resolved by C8 at the next connection like any other lost answer (C11 step 3). Nothing about the operation is lost by not raising.
- **The transport's next report is the verdict, whichever arrives first.** A keepalive answered after the expiry (`Servers` [K2](Servers.md#21-the-break-reporting-mechanism)'s second mechanism) says the line carried the silence, so the silence is the supervisor's: the observed value moves to *the supervisor not answering* and the condition is raised then — at most one keepalive interval and its exchange late, on `Servers` K3's own figures. A break declared instead (`Servers` K3) says the silence is the line's: the observation is discarded and no condition is raised. **The request's own answer, arriving late, is a third arrival and it discards the observation outright. Added 2026-08-29 at the re-gate**, the enumeration having named two and left the third to be read into it: a supervisor that answers after the waiter gave up proves the silence was neither the line's nor the supervisor's but the deadline's, so the held observation is discarded and nothing is raised — whichever of the three arrives first. **And the first of the three rests on a claim about the transport rather than on an observation of it**, that a keepalive answered after the expiry establishes the line carried the earlier request. **Label: unresolved**, at [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 23, which states what breaks. **The verdict itself does not move.**
- **The wait is bounded by `Servers`' declaration machinery and adds nothing of its own.** One keepalive interval where the line is alive, the declaration bound where it is not; the keepalive already runs on a fixed interval with no work in hand (`Servers` K2), so no timer, no watcher and no request is added — [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#84-nothing-is-polled-to-withdraw-a-record) §8.4's prohibition untouched. And the rule needs no figure for any waiter's deadline, which C16 deliberately leaves per-waiter.
- **Neither condition is inferred from the other, which is `Desktop app and delivery` V1's rule kept rather than bent.** The transport's report decides only whether the question was carried; what the supervisor is stays the command path's own observation. `Servers` [K12](Servers.md#23-what-is-never-used-for-this)'s opposite direction — a supervisor that does not answer is never read as a broken connection — stands exactly as written.
- **A condition already standing when a break is declared stands through the outage, and truthfully.** It was raised on a carried silence — the supervisor was not answering while SSH was live — and it is withdrawn on the supervisor answering, which C11 step 3's identity request is the first chance at after repair. Two records standing at once are presented as two (`Desktop app and delivery` [M7a](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#162-row-285-presented-a-live-condition-and-a-recorded-ending)); what this clause removes is only the verdict that was never earned.

**C18. A deadline is never a substitute for the connection's own break detection.** Fact 57's spread is why: a caller's own line pulled was detected in 56 seconds with keepalives on and **not detected at all for 21 minutes 45 seconds** with them off, and *"one provider answers no ping at all"* while its connection works perfectly, so **no health check in this document pings**. The connection's observed value comes from the mechanism `Servers` chooses under [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#85-the-connection-condition-needs-a-break-reported-without-using-the-connection) §8.5's constraint, and this document reads it rather than deriving one.

**C19. A transfer channel's deadline is on progress and not on completion, and what it measures is the loop's own attempt to move bytes rather than bytes moved.** A large file over a 1.2–1.3 MB/s uplink takes many minutes legitimately (Fact 25), so a completion deadline would fire on healthy work. **Bytes moved will not do either**, because drawing nothing is a normal and correct state of a channel under the shared budget ([R7](#82-the-enforcement-point-is-smoozys-own-byte-loop-and-the-evidence-forced-it)): at a low cap divided across several admitted channels a healthy channel can sit a long time between writes, and by byte accounting alone it is indistinguishable from one that has stalled. **So a channel blocked on the shared budget is not inactive, and the deadline runs only while the channel holds an allowance it cannot spend** — the loop drew from the budget and the write is not completing. On that clock it is set well above the longest legitimate stall observed: *"a frozen transfer sat at 17,233,920 bytes for 11.1 seconds while unfinished"* (Fact 20). **Expiry ends the channel and the record says *stalled*** (C17, [S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)) — **its own ending from 2026-08-31; this said *interrupted*, which is the one word that means the work may be carried on, and nothing here can carry it on.** No drop occurred, so no replaced connection ever runs [C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4 over it, and a row left saying *interrupted* would wait for a connection that never went away. Trying again is a new transfer with a new identity, exactly as at [S24](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when). And so that *waiting on the budget* is itself bounded rather than open, [Section 11](#11-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) hands the budget's holder the requirement that every admitted channel's draw is bounded below by a non-zero share.

### 4.7 Cancellation, and there are two of them

**C20. Cancelling a request whose acknowledgement is outstanding is not a thing this protocol offers.** A request cannot be un-sent; the pending-command record stands and C8 resolves it. Anything that presented such a cancel would have to claim the work did not happen, which is exactly what C10 says nothing can establish.

**C21. Stopping work the machine has taken is its own command, with its own operation identity, naming the target's** ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#53-the-deliberate-stop) §5.3 step 1). It is not a property of the original request, and it is subject to C4 like any other request. The stop sequence, the execution-group validation and the per-kind signal target are `Rented-server runtime`'s and the owning kind's document's.

**C22. Stopping a transfer is this document's, because the byte loop is this document's.** A stop closes the channel and stops the loop. It needs no machine-side stop command **before hand-over**, because nothing on the machine is running work for it. **After hand-over the transfer-support worker's termination is this document's through staging and cleanup** ([The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server)), which means the stop is the staging and cleanup rule of [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) rather than a signal contract; no signal contract in this project is universal across worker kinds.

**C23. A stop and a drop are never told apart from the wire, so the record is written before the stop is issued.** Fact 72 is the reason — damage in the pipe presents as a dropped connection — and the consequence is sharp, because [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) does opposite things in the two cases: a stop removes the staged material, a drop keeps it. **The durable record of which one Smoozy did is written before the channel is closed**, or a process that dies mid-stop leaves material the next connection would resume as though the user had never stopped it.

**C23a. What it looks like when that rule is not followed, observed 2026-08-22.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md) killed the machine-side session serving a moving transfer from a second connection. The drop arrived as `OSError: Socket is closed` **raised out of the write call**, propagated **past** the code that classifies the ending, and the process exited by traceback. **The durable record was left saying the transfer was still moving rather than interrupted.** **So a reader could not tell an interrupted transfer from one still in flight** — which is precisely the confusion [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) does opposite things on either side of. **The rule this earns is that the classification is not a step at the end of the loop**: the drop arrives as an exception from inside the write, so the record must be written by something that survives an exception raised anywhere in the transfer, not by a line the exception can jump over.

---

## 5. The bootstrap-and-repair request and package inspection

**Constraint:** [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) — *"The same surface carries one narrow, versioned bootstrap-and-repair request independent of the supervisor's machine-local endpoint. The request is authenticated by the SSH connection and carries a correlation identity, an idempotency key, and the requested package version."* Everything the machine does in response is `Rented-server runtime`'s.

**B1. The request is one request, and its narrowness is the point.** It carries the protocol version (P2), the **correlation identity**, the **idempotency key** — which is that same operation identity, per C1 — and one verified **release descriptor**: manifest format and release sequence, requested package version, manifest digest, selected artifact identity, byte length and SHA-256 digest. It has no variants, no sub-commands and no mode flag: *place it, repair it, or replace it* is one request whose answer depends on what the machine is found holding, and the finding is `Rented-server runtime`'s. No request can select bytes by a version string alone.

**B2. It rides on the pre-package surface and never on the machine-local endpoint, and no Smoozy program receives it on the machine.** The request is the desktop's own framed, durably recorded unit of work, and its machine-side steps are performed through the surface's two mechanisms: artifact bytes and durable records move as subsystem protocol fields under [Section 7](#7-staging-integrity-resume-and-publication--one-contract-in-both-directions)'s contract, and the command channel carries only [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)-admissible commands — boot registration and `Server Setup`'s elevated steps are the ones this transaction needs, and each is constant or carries its value by the rule's routes. So the request reaches a machine whose supervisor is dead, whose package is half-placed, or that has never had a package at all — all three by the same route. Where a verified runtime is alive, `Rented-server runtime` may perform its own half of the transaction; where none is, the desktop performs it through the surface, and the transaction's shape is that document's either way. P4 is why it is framed here rather than by the adapter it may be replacing.

**B3. This is the one request in this document whose durable record is not the supervisor's, and the reason is that the supervisor may not exist when it arrives.** Its idempotency is answered from the **durable package-phase record**, which `Rented-server runtime` owns and which the shape already requires to hold *"whether retry continues an interrupted attempt or starts a fresh replacement"*. C4's rule is unchanged — the identity is recorded durably before the act — and only the record it is recorded in differs. **A builder must not look for an operation record for this request.**

**B4. Package inspection compares content, never presence — and it is a composed subsystem reading in both phases.** Its values are the installation's durable records, read at installation-owned paths over the file-transfer subsystem, whose paths are protocol fields; the desktop composes the answer, and no machine-side program answers it. That is what lets inspection answer on a machine that has never held a package — every record absent, reported honestly as that — and beside a dead supervisor alike, with one route and no phase seam. The recycle observation forbids the other reading in the plainest possible terms: the boot hook *"was not deleted but replaced"* by an 82-byte provider default dated at the recycle moment, so *"a check for 'does my hook exist' passes while the hook does nothing Smoozy asked for"*. **So inspection returns the active-package pointer's release sequence, package version, manifest digest and artifact digest; the boot-registration record's own content; the phase record's phase and retained-adapter intervals; the installation identity; and each adapter's declared protocol interval and request set** — and a reading in which any of those is present but not Smoozy's is the *present and different* branch of C14, not a healthy machine.

**B5. Inspection is a reading and its answer is never durable state on the desktop side.** [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#3-desired-state-and-observed-state) §3's D4 and D5 bind it: it carries the moment it was taken and the clock it was taken on, and where it is remembered at all it carries the installation identity it was read under and is **subject-held** on the server row.

**B6. What the request's result says about a commit blocked by live work is carried and not interpreted here.** The shape settles that the whole commit is blocked while live or held work depends on the runtime, and *"the caller is told that the upgrade is deferred for that reason"*. This document carries that as one of the request's ordinary results — it is not a failure, it does not expire a waiter, and it does not license a second attempt under a fresh identity.

**B7. Nothing about this request is automatic in this document.** The automatic supervisor-unavailable repair trigger is `Desktop app and delivery`'s and the user-facing fallback re-run is `Server Setup`'s ([The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server)). What is owned here is the request they both send.

**B8. A server package is selected from a signed release manifest, not from a filename or a version endpoint.** The manifest's canonical bytes carry: manifest format and monotonically increasing release sequence; package version; the current protocol version and its one immediately previous supported version where one exists; and, for every artifact, a stable artifact identity, supported operating-system family, processor architecture and ABI/capability tuple, byte length, SHA-256 digest, fixed entry-point name, and exact HTTPS source or sources. `Server Setup` supplies the detected rental tuple; the desktop selects an artifact only on an exact manifest match. The set of tuples the product supports is the Creator-ruling boundary outside this document; **no closest-architecture, closest-ABI or generic-Linux fallback is permitted**. No matching entry is an unsupported machine reported before any package byte is executed.

**B8a. Who serves a released server package. Added 2026-09-06, outside any round. Label: Creator-stated (2026-09-06).** *(Heading narrowed 2026-10-24 by step 11's plan pass. It read ~~Who serves a released server package, and it is not who serves the desktop application.~~ Every release already publishes the desktop artifacts to this same repository, and `Desktop app and delivery` E10a now serves the desktop's update file from it too. What stays different is the act — the three bullets below — and the trust root (`State, identity and recovery` J10).)* [B8](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) requires every artifact entry to carry an exact HTTPS source or sources, and until this clause no document in this stage said who serves one. **A released server-package artifact, the signed release manifest that names it, and the root-signed key-set statement [B9](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) distributes with that manifest are served from a separate public repository the Creator owns, and from nowhere else.** Their own words: *"it will be fetched there from a separate public repo"*, with *"smoozy.app, which I own"* linking to it. **Public is the load-bearing half rather than the repository being a repository**: [B10](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) fetches with an address and nothing else, so a source that will not answer an unauthenticated request cannot be named in a manifest at all.

**The exact address is the Creator-ruling boundary outside this document, in the same way B8 already makes the supported tuple set one.** They stated the same day that the repository's name was not settled — *"called something like Smoozy-v2-Releases or similar, I haven't decided yet"* — so **no address, no name and no rule for composing one is written here or anywhere downstream of here.** A manifest carries the source the Creator gives it, as a value. Nothing derives a source from a version, a name or a pattern: that is B8's own prohibition for the artifact, applied to the address.

**What carries across from [Desktop app and delivery](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#193-hosting-and-what-does-not-go-up-with-a-request) §19.3 is cited and not restated, a second copy of any of it being a copy that drifts from the clause that owns it.** The no-account promise is that document's E10 — *"No account, no registration, no email"* — and it binds this fetch exactly as it binds the desktop application's. E11's rule that nothing about the user goes up with the request binds with it. Where the cost falls is E13's — *"Hosting cost falls on the Creator rather than on the user"* — and this clause is not a second ruling on it. E14 is untouched: which tool runs the pipeline is still an intention rather than a decision, and nothing here depends on which it is.

**Three things make this a different act from a person fetching the desktop application, and they are why §19 could not simply be pointed at.**

- **Nobody is present.** The desktop application is fetched by a person with a browser, following a link from the Creator's own website. A server package is fetched by the desktop application itself, from an address B10 reads out of a manifest it has already verified — no page, no link, no person and no choice at the moment of fetching.
- **The seller's copy is not a source for this.** The Creator's second place — *"Lemon Squeezy will have a separate section where I will have to upload the program to"* — is a route for a person who has bought, and a route reached by buying is not an address an unauthenticated fetch can name. **A server package has the public repository and nothing beside it**, which is also what keeps E10's promise reachable here. *(Added 2026-10-27, from the Creator's decision of 2026-10-04: Smoozy is now free software and is not sold, so the Creator's quoted seller half here and in [B8b](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) no longer applies while the quote stays as the record; the releases repository does not move, since every released desktop reads its own release there under [B8d](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection). No auditor of this document has seen this note.)*
- **The bytes are not for the computer that fetches them.** B10 downloads to the desktop, verifies the artifact's byte length and digest there, and only then uploads to a machine that holds nothing of Smoozy's and runs nothing of Smoozy's until the verified entry point ([Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 17). **The rented machine never reaches the source and is given no route to one**: B10 puts both ends of the verification on the desktop, and a machine fetching its own package would be choosing its own bytes on a machine [A6](#3-the-authentication-boundary) trusts about itself not at all.

**B8b. Where a desktop finds the current release, and it is one fixed address. Added 2026-09-19, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build. Label: documented (GitHub's own pages, read 2026-09-19) for every fact about GitHub; the rest is Smoozy's own decision.** [B9](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) says *"a build never has to be told about a release made after it"*, and until this clause no document in this stage said where a desktop finds the current one. **The desktop reads the current release file — the one file carrying the signed release manifest and the root-signed key-set statement B9 distributes with it — from the repository's fixed latest address, `https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>/releases/latest/download/<file>`, and from nowhere else.** The repository is the one [B8a](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) names, and it is on GitHub — Creator-stated 2026-09-06, in the same words B8a quotes from: *"two places: lemon Squeezy and GitHub"*. GitHub's *Linking to releases* page documents this form for *"a download of your latest release asset that was manually uploaded"*, which the release file is. **The owner and repository name stay the Creator's, outside this document, exactly as B8a leaves them**, and the file name is Build's. **This narrows one sentence of B8a and says so**: B8a writes no rule for composing an address, and this clause writes one fixed suffix onto the repository's own address. It carries no version, no sequence and nothing derived from either, so B8's prohibition on deriving a source from a version, a name or a pattern still holds whole — every artifact source is still a value the manifest carries.

**It trusts nothing about the answer beyond what B9 and B10 already check, and that is enough.** Whatever the address serves goes through B10's checks before any artifact is downloaded — the key-set statement's root signature and non-rollback rules, the manifest signature by an allowed non-revoked online key, the manifest's canonical digest — so the one thing the address can be wrong about is which release is latest. **A wrong latest can only hand the desktop an older signed release**, which B9's floors refuse where its sequence is below the floor in force, and which is otherwise a genuine release that is merely not the newest. It can never hand over an unsigned release or a rolled-back one, because neither passes B9 and B10 whoever served it. The address is not a trust anchor and gains no check of its own.

**Two duties fall on the side that publishes a release.** Every release is published **explicitly marked latest**, and **never as a draft or a pre-release**. GitHub's *REST API endpoints for releases* page: *"Drafts and prereleases cannot be set as latest."* Its *Managing releases in a repository* page: where *Set as latest release* is not selected, *"the latest release label will automatically be assigned based on semantic versioning"*, and no page read says how that ranks this project's release names.

**NARROWED 2026-09-28 BY [B8d](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection): no desktop reads this address any more.** A desktop places the server package of its own release and reads that release's file from that release's own address. **This clause stays for the one reader it still has**: the release runner, which reads the last published release's numbers off it before raising B9's floors (`Preparation/Groundwork/Templates/Release Runner.md` step 3a). Its two duties stay for that reader, and nothing a user runs depends on them. *(No auditor of this document has seen this note.)*

**What is unproved is written as unproved.** GitHub's pages give three rules for *latest* — the website's label, the API's `make_latest` setting, and the API's latest-release endpoint, which sorts by the date of the release's commit — and **no page read says which of them the `/releases/latest/download/` address follows, or whether that address answers with the file or with a redirect.** Both are left unproved: the two duties are what keep all three rules on the release just published, and the paragraph above is why a failure of them does no harm. **They are watched once, at the first publish**, by fetching the address and checking that the release sequence it serves is the one just published. **A redirect is followed over HTTPS only.**

**B8c. The machine program's artifact needs no C library from the machine, because the manifest has nowhere to write a floor. Added 2026-09-26, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build. Label: observed (on a rented machine, 2026-09-23) for the fault; documented (each publication named below, read 2026-09-23) for every fact about a library, a toolchain or a runner; the rest is Smoozy's own decision.** B8's tuple names an operating-system family, a processor architecture and an ABI word, and **it carries no release of anything** — and B8 itself forbids the closest-ABI and generation arithmetic a floor would need. `Server Setup` [M1a](Server%20Setup.md#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each) names **Ubuntu and Debian and no version of either**. So the promise an artifact entry has to keep is *every* Ubuntu and *every* Debian the tuple selects it for, and **an artifact linked against a release of the machine's own C library cannot keep that promise at any release**: the library records, for every symbol the program imports, the release it came from, and a machine with an older one refuses to start the program. **This is what happened.** Release 1638's artifact was built on a runner carrying release 2.39; a rented Ubuntu 22.04 machine carries 2.35; the placed program died five times in one second with *"version `GLIBC_2.39' not found"* and the setup could not finish (observed — `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Piece 12, the fifth pass — Sesterce.md`). The distributions' own package pages give the releases: Ubuntu 22.04 `2.35-0ubuntu3.15`, Ubuntu 24.04 `2.39-0ubuntu8.9`, Debian 12 `2.36-9+deb12u14`, Debian 11 `2.31-13+deb11u14` (documented). **So by the release it had, the artifact ran on Ubuntu 24.04 and newer only, and Ubuntu 22.04 — in standard support until May 2027 — and both Debian releases were out.**

**The rule: the artifact carries its own C library inside it and asks the machine for none.** It is built for the target whose C runtime Rust links statically by default — the Rust Reference lists `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` among the targets *"which are static by default"* (documented) — and **the release tool refuses to sign an artifact whose bytes name any release of the machine's C library**, the same reading the build workflow makes and fails a run on. **What does not move is the tuple and the identity**: the entry is still `linux-x86_64-gnu`, still selected by a machine whose C library is the GNU one, exactly as [Server Setup](Server%20Setup.md)'s reading composes it. The word says which machines the entry is *for*, and a program that needs nothing of that library runs on all of them. Changing the word would make every machine on record stop matching and every desktop already in the world stop finding a package; keeping it changes nothing any machine or desktop reads. **What was weighed and not taken**: building on an older runner image. It buys a floor at that image's release — still not *every* release — and GitHub's own images page says it *"only support[s] the latest 2 versions of an OS"* (documented), so the floor would rise under a shipped product the day that image retires.

**What the check is worth, stated rather than overstated.** It reads the bytes for the text the library's versioned imports leave. It catches the fault that happened, at the desk, before a signature. **It does not run the program on anything**: it says nothing of the oldest kernel the program needs, nothing of a library a future dependency might open by name at run time, and nothing of the desktop application's own artifacts, which this manifest does not carry. **What it costs**: a larger artifact and a program that no longer shares the machine's C library — both measured at Build, and written in that step's account.

**B8d. A desktop places the server package of its own release, and of no other. Added 2026-09-28, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build. Label: Creator-stated (2026-09-28) for the rule, carried from their ruling on the application's own update; observed (Build's release accounts) and documented (GitHub's pages, read 2026-09-19) for the address; the rest is Smoozy's own decision. No auditor of this document has seen it.** Until this clause a desktop fetched the *latest* published release (B8b), so a desktop not yet updated — a Linux user who declined, or an update held by `Desktop app and delivery` Z31 — placed a program newer than itself, and two protocol versions apart every repair placed the same program it could not talk to. **So: every desktop build places the server package published in the same release as itself.** *Older* then has one meaning — the machine's active release sequence (B4) is below the desktop's own — and no desktop ever places a program newer than itself.

- **How a build names its own release.** By its **package version**, which every build already carries about itself; nothing is derived from a commit or a count. The release file is read from **that release's own address** on the repository B8a names — `https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>/releases/download/<tag>/<file>`, where the tag is the release's own and names its package version. **The tag's exact form, like the file name, is Build's**, and the release runner publishes under it. **That form is observed**: the release runner fetches each release by exactly this address after publishing it, and every release since the fifth was verified that way (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/Piece 11 — the fifth release.md` and after). GitHub's *REST API endpoints for releases* page shows the same form in its example answer, *"https://github.com/octocat/Hello-World/releases/download/v1.0.0/example.zip"*, which is an example rather than a stated rule (documented, read 2026-09-19).
- **This narrows B8a's sentence a second time and says so.** B8a forbids deriving a source from a version; B8b wrote a fixed suffix carrying no version. This clause writes one address carrying the build's own package version, for the release file only. **Every artifact source is still a value the verified manifest carries**, and B8b's reasoning holds unchanged: the address is not a trust anchor, and whatever it serves goes through B9 and B10.
- **What is accepted.** A release file whose manifest names the build's own package version, and whose release sequence then passes B9's floors — both unchanged. **The desktop's own release sequence is that manifest's**, kept with the server-package trust record once verified (`State, identity and recovery` J6), and it is what P3b and `Desktop app and delivery` V11 compare a machine against. **A copy whose remembered floor is above its own release refuses its own manifest** and places nothing; only a copy that accepted a newer release by B8b's address before this clause can reach that state, and the ordinary verification failure is what it reports. Its update ends it.
- **Whether the protocol check at placement still earns its place: it does not, as a check of its own.** A desktop that placed the latest release had to ask whether it could talk to what it placed. Under this clause it places the release built from its own commit, whose protocol interval the release tool takes from the same build (`Preparation/Groundwork/Templates/Release Runner.md`, step 1: one clean, pushed commit). **The manifest's own package version matching the build's is that check**, and a second comparison would only catch a release built from two commits, which the release runner already refuses.
- **Every release's files stay reachable for as long as a desktop of that release can be in use**, which is for good — nothing forces a desktop to update (Linux's declined box). **So a published release is never deleted and its files are never replaced**: a duty on the side that publishes, written into the release runner's brief. GitHub's *About releases* page: *"There is no limit on the total size of a release, nor bandwidth usage"* (documented, read 2026-09-19), so keeping them costs nothing. **Deleting a release is the one thing that breaks this**, and it is the Creator's own act. A desktop that meets its own release missing places nothing and reports the fetch failure.
- **A development build under B10a is untouched.** It places a program built on the same computer and reads no release at all.

**B9. Trust is anchored independently of the downloaded bytes.** The desktop carries an offline release-root public key, **and beside it two built-in floor values: the greatest key-set generation and the greatest release sequence published when that build was made**. A root-signed key-set statement names the allowed online manifest keys, its own increasing generation, each key's validity from a release sequence, and every revocation with the first disallowed release sequence. The desktop durably remembers the greatest accepted key-set generation and release sequence and refuses rollback of either. **On each of the two numbers the floor actually in force is the greater of the built-in value and the remembered one, never the smaller** — so a remembered floor is never lowered by a build, and a build never has to be told about a release made after it. An online-key transition is accepted when the newer root-signed statement authorizes it; a revoked online key cannot authorize a manifest at or after its revocation sequence. The statement and its root signature are distributed with the manifest and retained across missed releases, so a desktop need not have observed an overlap. Replacing the offline root itself is a desktop-delivery trust change, not a server-package download.

**Why the built-in pair is part of this clause rather than an addition to it. Added 2026-09-06, out of round 1 of step 8a's code.** Without it every floor this clause gives comes out of the desktop's own memory of a release it has already accepted, so **a copy that has never accepted one has no floor at all** — which is every install on its first day, and every install whose server-package trust record [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) J7 has discarded. Such a copy admits any root-signed statement the root has ever signed, at any generation, and then accepts any manifest an allowed key signed at any sequence below that key's revocation: **the revocation is real and the desktop simply never meets the statement carrying it.** The answer *the statement is distributed with the manifest* is a statement about who serves the bytes, and this clause opens by anchoring trust independently of them — so the disagreement was inside this clause rather than between two. With the pair, a revocation reaches every desktop by the route that already reaches every desktop, which is the desktop's own update, rather than by a route whoever serves the bytes controls. **J7's discard then falls back to the build's floor instead of to none**, which is what keeps J8's reasoning — that the discard is a correction and not a weakening — worth what it claims over the whole life of an install rather than only until the next release is accepted.

**The built-in pair is the last published release's generation and sequence, and never a value derived from the build itself.** It is raised by hand, before a release is built, in the same act that fixes that release's own sequence. **A commit count, a build number or any other derived value is forbidden here** for the reason B8 forbids deriving an artifact from a version string: a build carrying a floor above any release that exists refuses every genuine release, for every fresh install, until the next one is published. A build whose pair has never been raised carries zero and zero, refuses nothing on a number, and is exactly the state the first release meets.

**B10. Verification precedes execution at both ends of the transfer, and both ends are the desktop's.** Before download, the desktop verifies the root-signed key-set statement, its non-rollback rules, the manifest signature by an allowed non-revoked online key, and the manifest's canonical digest. It downloads only from an exact source named by that verified manifest and verifies the selected artifact's byte length and SHA-256 digest before upload. After upload, the staged artifact is verified **at rest, from the desktop**: its bytes are read back over the file-transfer subsystem and their byte length and SHA-256 digest computed in Smoozy's own loop and compared with B1's release descriptor, before the artifact is made executable — the subsystem's own attribute-setting operation, `SSH_FXP_SETSTAT` with the permissions attribute (`draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02` §6.9, read 2026-08-22: *"used for operations such as changing the ownership, permissions or access times"*), in [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)'s observed request set — and before any byte of it is invoked. The upload itself ran under [Section 7](#7-staging-integrity-resume-and-publication--one-contract-in-both-directions)'s contract, so the pass-through digest covered the pipe and the read-back covers rest — [S21](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)'s two stretches, both. **What the machine end is worth is stated rather than overstated: the read-back is served by the machine's own subsystem, so it detects corruption in transfer and at rest — Fact 72's observed failures — and defends against nothing the machine itself does.** No verifier running on a machine can exceed that machine's honesty, and the wrapper this clause used to assign the comparison to could not have either; there is no machine-side trust anchor and none is claimed. The anchors are B9's offline root and the connection [Section 3](#3-the-authentication-boundary) authenticates, and a machine is trusted about itself exactly as far as [A6](#3-the-authentication-boundary) says — which is not at all. The placement records the verified release sequence, manifest digest, artifact identity and artifact digest in the package-phase record — written through the same surface before hand-over, by the runtime afterwards — and validates those values on continuation. A signature, digest, tuple or non-rollback failure leaves the active pointer and boot registration untouched and returns a correlated verification failure; version equality never overrides it.

**B10a. A development build may place a server program built on the user's own computer, and no release build can. Added 2026-09-27, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build. Label: Creator-stated (2026-09-27).** **The authority is the Creator's ruling, and nothing else.** The build runner's ruling R5 (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The runner's rulings.md`) had refused a second signing root because it *"would change B9, a security clause, and that would go up rather than be taken here"*, and named the cost: nothing on a rented machine can be watched before a release is published. The Creator ruled on that cost on 2026-09-27, in their own words as the build board records them: *"We're testing on servers I genuinely don't care about, they are just playgrounds really"*, and *"it should be safe to run GitHub builds a little less frequently… I think we can speed things up."* The board took this as a ruling on two terms, and both are written here: the route is absent from every release build and proved absent, and **the last pass before any release still runs on a real published release**.

- **What it permits.** A development build of the desktop application, started with the development-only throwaway setting (the one the code marks against `Desktop app and delivery` F11, L3 and L3a) and pointed at a throwaway folder, may place a program built on the user's own computer in place of B8's selected artifact. It reads no manifest and fetches nothing: B8's selection and B8b's address are not reached.
- **Why no second key exists.** The program never left that computer before it is placed, so B9's anchor has nothing to anchor. **No second root is created and B9 is not widened**; R5 stands, and this clause is a narrow exception written beside B9 rather than a change to it.
- **What still runs.** B10's at-rest read-back runs unchanged, against that file's own byte length and SHA-256 digest, before the program is made executable and before any byte of it is invoked. Everything after the placement's fetch is unchanged.
- **What is neither read nor written.** The server-package trust record (`State, identity and recovery` J6) and B9's floors.
- **A development copy that was given a desk program takes that program as its own release for `Desktop app and delivery` V11, V12 and V14.** Its own release sequence is the desk program's zero, which the placement already records. So a machine the shortcut placed reads equal and is never updated, and a machine that any published release placed reads *too new* and is told so rather than replaced. A development copy given no desk program reads its own release under B8d like any build. *(Added 2026-09-28, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build. No auditor of this document has seen it.)*
- **No release build honours it, and that is proved rather than promised.** The route is absent from a release build, not switched off in it, and the proof is in the build: a test that asks in both builds, the release library's bytes read for the route's own argument, and the hosted workflow's check of each release binary.
- **It is not a fallback that skips a check** in `Desktop app and delivery` E15's words, because a release build has no route to it at all.
- **A pass on it proves nothing about a release.** It proves the desk-built program on a real machine and nothing about B8 to B10's fetch, signature or floors; which is why the last pass before any release runs on the real published release (`Preparation/Build/Plan.md` step 10c).

---

## 6. Transfer channels, concurrency and priority

**Constraint:** [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) — *"Transfer data moves on its own SSH channels"* — and Fact 25's measurements, quoted in *What binds this*.

### 6.1 A transfer is a channel, and the channel is independent of the command channel

**T1. Each transfer runs on its own file-transfer subsystem channel on the connection `Servers` holds.** No transfer shares a channel with another transfer or with the command channel, so a stalled or cancelled transfer cannot stall a command and a command cannot delay a transfer's bytes.

**T1 is a choice and not a protocol requirement, and a builder must not read it as forced — recorded 2026-08-21.** One channel can carry several files at once. `draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02`, the version-3 protocol this document's [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) observed, says *"There is no limit on the number of outstanding (non-acknowledged) requests that the client may send to the server"* and identifies every request by a client-chosen `id`, and nothing in it binds one open handle to one channel. Posed on the Creator's Mac on 2026-08-20 against `/usr/libexec/sftp-server` over a pipe: two files opened with no close between them, eight writes interleaved across the two handles and all sent before any reply was read, every reply status 0, each file holding only its own bytes. **What T1 buys is the isolation stated above and nothing else**, so a machine met at a session cap too low for one channel per transfer has a fallback nobody has had to name, and [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 5's cost is smaller than it reads.

**T2. Concurrent subsystem channels on one connection are documented, and the cap is the server's to set.** Documented — `sshd_config(5)`, macOS 26.5 man page dated October 4, 2025, read 2026-08-17: `MaxSessions` *"Specifies the maximum number of open shell, login or subsystem (e.g. sftp) sessions permitted per network connection. Multiple sessions may be established by clients that support connection multiplexing. Setting MaxSessions to 1 will effectively disable session multiplexing, whereas setting it to 0 will prevent all shell, login and subsystem sessions while still permitting forwarding. The default is 10."* **So the pool size is a request rather than a guarantee**: a rental may permit fewer sessions than Smoozy asks for, and the setting is not readable before it is hit. **A refused channel reduces the learned cap and is never by itself a transfer failure.** When the learned cap is one, the primary connection uses T5's alternating reading/command lease and every transfer uses an additional authenticated connection that is transfer-only for its life. A transfer connection never takes a command or browsing-reading session, so an arriving command cannot be starved by transfer bytes and never requires a second session on that connection. `Servers` owns opening and replacing that additional connection; this document owns its single transfer-only use. The note under T1 establishes that sharing one subsystem channel is possible, but isolation remains this document's declared route rather than an improvised fallback.

**T3. A transfer is bound to the server it was started against and to nothing else.** Not to the browsing position, not to a screen, not to a connection. That is the primitive Row 185 needs and `File Browser` writes against.

**T4. A transfer has a transfer identity**, issued by the initiating side when the transfer is arranged and held at both ends ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#22-the-register-of-identities) §2.2). It is what the staging path is synthesised from ([7.2](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it)) and what a resumed transfer is matched by. **A transfer identity is not an operation identity**; where a transfer is work the machine took — a transfer-support worker after hand-over — that work has its own operation identity beside it.

### 6.2 Concurrency, and one admission point rather than one per server

**T5. Every session on a connection is admitted through one point per server, and every per-server point is admitted through one desktop-side point.** **Every session, and not only the transfer channels**: `MaxSessions` is one pool per connection, and this document draws on it from three places — a transfer channel (T1), a command invocation ([1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) item 1, one per request) and the reading channel ([1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) item 2). At a learned cap above one, the point reserves one slot for commands. **At a learned cap of one, reserve arithmetic is forbidden:** the primary connection holds a single lease which alternates between the reading subsystem and a command invocation. A queued command closes the reading channel after its current framed reply, takes the one session, completes or records its interrupted outcome, and then permits the reading channel to reopen; no second reading begins while a command waits. **Closing that channel destroys every handle open on it**, and what the operations holding one do about it is [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) item 2's — the lease does not wait for a handle to close, deliberately, because a walk may hold a directory handle for as long as it likes and the command route may not be made to wait on it. Transfers take only T2's additional transfer-only connection and cannot acquire the primary lease. Thus a long-lived reading cannot make command capacity zero, and the command route remains available without asking one connection for two sessions. The per-server point is what makes a per-server pool a pool rather than a race. The desktop-side point is forced by measurement rather than chosen: a whole second rented machine bought about **20%, not 100%**, *"the slowest set the finish time"* with the line idle for 80 of 112 seconds, and uncoordinated upload fan-out was **actively worse** than one machine at a time — 1.54, 1.24, 0.99 MB/s across one, two and three boxes (Fact 25). **So a pool sized per server and admitted per server tunes the wrong thing**, and admission has to know about every server at once, beside the one shared rate budget `Desktop app and delivery` holds.

**T5a. The two points have different scopes, and this clause says so rather than leaving *every session* to be read twice. Added 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, routed by the runner from [Desktop app and delivery](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#22-unresolved) §22 item 31.** **The per-server point counts every session on that server's connections** — transfer channel, command invocation and reading channel alike — and that is what *every session, and not only the transfer channels* fixes, because `MaxSessions` is one pool per connection and all three draw on it. **The desktop-side point is a different instrument with a different job**: it is the one place that knows about every server at once, and what it exists to hold down is **line contention**, which Fact 25 measured and which is made by bytes rather than by sessions. So it admits what moves bytes across the line — transfer channels and the update fetch — and does **not** gate a command invocation or a reading channel. **The reason is not economy, it is correctness**: gating a command behind desktop-wide transfer load would put a stop, a query or a hold-release on every server at once behind exactly the load that T5's reserved command slot — or, at a cap of one, its alternating lease — exists to keep it clear of. **Neither point weakens the other**: the per-server point still counts all three kinds against one pool, and either point may refuse.

**T6. The measured shape of the useful range is supplied and the number is not picked here.** Observed: the knee is at **two streams** for download — one to two buys 53%, two to four buys 3%, four to eight buys 11% — and around **four** for upload, where the uplink saturates near 1.85–1.9 MB/s, and *"most of the gain is hiding a flat 1.0–1.4 s connection setup rather than adding bandwidth"* (Fact 25). Down is **11–12 times** up. **Rows 188 and 206 pick the pool size** and are `File Browser`'s and `Download`'s; what is supplied is that the range is two to four, that it differs by direction, and that a number tuned above it buys setup-hiding rather than throughput.

**T7. A single transfer is already internally pipelined, and that is not the same as concurrency.** Documented — `sftp(1)`, macOS 26.5 man page dated December 6, 2024, read 2026-08-17: `nrequests` *"Controls how many concurrent SFTP read or write requests may be in progress at any point in time during a download or upload. By default 64 requests may be active concurrently"*, and `buffer` *"Controls the maximum buffer size for a single SFTP read/write operation used during download or upload. By default a 32KB buffer is used."* **So depth inside one channel and count of channels are two separate dials**, and Fact 25's two-to-four is the second of them. A builder that raises the first believing it is raising the second has changed nothing Fact 25 measured.

### 6.3 Priority, and what is owned here is the mechanism

**T8. Every transfer carries a declared priority class, and admission is by class then by arrival.** The class is declared by the module that arranged the transfer, in its own document, per action — the same discipline [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#92-three-tests-applied-in-order-and-the-answer-is-declared-rather-than-inferred) §9.2 requires of a partition unit. **Nothing decides a class at run time**, because a class decided at run time is a class two callers can decide differently.

**T9. The relative order of the classes is not settled here.** Rows 189 and 207 own it — `File Browser`'s and `Download`'s — and Row 189 is answered at `File Browser` §6.2, the spec's own marker having been cleared on 2026-08-23 in favour of the settled order it states. *(Until 2026-10-24 this said "Row 189 is the marker naming Architecture", the marker written in its bracketed form.)* What is supplied is that there is one order, one admission point per server under T5, and that the moment the order actually bites is a repair: a single repaired connection can resume auto-downloads, manual downloads and uploads across several servers at once ([4.4](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4).

**T10. Admission never starves a class indefinitely.** A class that is admitted only when no higher class is waiting is a class that never runs while an auto-download arrangement exists, and an arrangement *"keeps trying, and never switches itself off"* (Row 219). So the order is a preference within each admission decision and not a gate: every class is guaranteed a share of the per-server pool, and **the share is Rows 189 and 207's to declare against this mechanism**.

### 6.4 The transfer-support worker's declarations, which are this document's and had not been made

**T11. The transfer-support kind's five declarations, against [Rented-server runtime](Rented-server%20runtime.md#71-what-each-owning-document-declares) A1, none decided at run time. Added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close gate repair, routed by the runner from that document's [§16](Rented-server%20runtime.md#16-unresolved) item 28.** Its [W1](Rented-server%20runtime.md#51-the-seven-kinds-and-which-document-owns-each) names this document the owner of the kind's declarations and **this document had made none**, so **the kind was arbitrated under A3's default** — *"it takes no resource exclusively — the processors and the machine's memory shared, no card — it is ranked last, and it is treated as not cancellable"*. Two of those three are what this document would have declared anyway; **the third is false of this kind**, because C22 gives it a termination contract, so W2's *cancellation is refused* never applied to it and a default that says otherwise misdescribes it.

| Declaration | Value |
|---|---|
| resource class | the processors and the machine's memory **shared**; **no card**. The worker runs the byte loop, [7.6](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)'s digest and [7.5](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s single publication, and takes nothing else A1 arbitrates: **the disk and the connection are not on A1's contended list**, that clause saying in as many words that *"transfer concurrency and priority are ... §6's, and nothing here duplicates that admission point"* |
| priority | **below the render kind; above the preview and media kinds.** Below the render because `Rendering` K1 ranks it first and `Queue & History`'s allotment gives it the cards; above the derivative kinds because a transfer is what a render's material arrives on and what a finished job's outputs leave on, and `Job files and outputs` B24 already declares a derivative *never blocking a render* rather than competing for that place. **The rank barely bites**: A5's test is resources, this class holds none exclusively, and A8 makes priority a preference within an admission decision rather than a gate |
| cancellable | **at any moment**, the termination being [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s staging and cleanup rather than a signal ([C22](#47-cancellation-and-there-are-two-of-them), and that document's X14), and subject to [C23](#47-cancellation-and-there-are-two-of-them)'s ordering — the durable record of which of S26's five endings Smoozy took is written **before** the channel is closed. **A cancellation the arbitration mechanism itself performs does not arise:** its A6 cancels only an operation holding what an exclusive operation needs, and this class holds nothing exclusively |
| atomic or partial | **atomic**, the unit being one transfer. [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#92-three-tests-applied-in-order-and-the-answer-is-declared-rather-than-inferred) §9.2 **test 2**, which [7.1](#71-the-contract-stated-once)'s *why clause 1 is absolute* paragraph already states *"for this document's own unit"*: a half-arrived transfer at the final name is Fact 18's hybrid, and staging plus one publication is the only arrangement that prevents it. **Staged material kept for a resume is not a partial unit** — the final name is published or it is not (7.1 clause 1) |
| exclusion | **nothing declared, which A1 reads as unlimited.** What bounds machine-side transfer concurrency is [6.2](#62-concurrency-and-one-admission-point-rather-than-one-per-server)'s two admission points, T6's measured two-to-four range and Rows 188 and 206's pool sizes — all of them on the caller's side, and the machine never sees more transfers than they let through. **A declaration of one would cap machine-side transfers at one** and contradict every one of those, so it is not made. `Rented-server runtime` A5a is the rule that acts on this declaration, and it is not reached by this kind |

**T11a. The two admission points are different instruments and neither stands in for the other.** [T5](#62-concurrency-and-one-admission-point-rather-than-one-per-server)'s points admit **sessions** against `MaxSessions` and **bytes** against the line, on the desktop side; `Rented-server runtime` A4's is one point per **machine** admitting **worker starts** against the contended resources. A transfer after hand-over passes both — T5's for its channel, A4's for its worker — and neither figure is derived from the other. **What is not declared here is the termination contract's fourth named thing**, which [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 20 carries.

---

## 7. Staging, integrity, resume and publication — one contract in both directions

**S0a. Transfers presentation has one durable desktop ordering stamp per displayed request or arrangement. Added 2026-10-23 for seventeenth note 8, on the runner’s ruling.** On first starting an upload, dependency upload, manual download or auto-download arrangement, its owner saves `started_at_utc` from the desktop wall clock and `start_order` from one desktop-wide monotonically allocated sequence, atomically with that parent record. File units do not receive new row-order stamps. Resuming, waiting, reconnecting, restarting the application and adding files to an arrangement preserve both. Switching off an arrangement and starting another allocates a new pair. The common sequence is allocated transactionally through the existing local-record writer, across all kinds and servers, and is retained beyond parent expiry; it is not a comparison of per-table row numbers. Presentation partitions running/waiting from terminal rows, then sorts known starts by descending `(started_at_utc, start_order)`. Display the saved instant in desktop local time with date and seconds. A wall-clock correction may affect chronological placement; never rewrite the saved instant to conceal it. Legacy parents lacking a start remain unknown, follow known starts in their group, and use their stable request identity to break ties; show *Start time not recorded*. Do not invent a time from a server clock or migration time. This adds fields to existing parent records, not a new transfer class or lifetime. Upload and dependency-upload owners take this same supply. No auditor has seen it.

**Constraint:** [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) — *"`Server protocol and transfers` defines one transfer staging-and-publication contract in both directions for the whole of the machine's life: part-arrived bytes never sit at the final name; integrity and resume are measured against staged material; publication is the last step; and abandoned staging is cleaned up. The pre-package surface performs that contract before hand-over, the transfer-support worker performs it afterwards, and the contract does not change at hand-over."* And Row 281.

### 7.1 The contract, stated once

**Four clauses, and they hold in both directions, before and after hand-over, for every module named in the scope.**

1. **Part-arrived bytes never sit at the final name.** Every byte of every transfer is written to a **staging path** this document names, and the final name holds either the previous file or nothing until publication.
2. **Integrity and resume are measured against staged material**, never against the final name and never against a file's presence, name or size — which is [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#13-rules-that-bind-every-store) §1.3 rule 5 applied to material.
3. **Publication is the last step**, and it is one step ([7.5](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)).
4. **Abandoned staging is cleaned up** ([7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)).

**Why clause 1 is absolute rather than a matter of degree.** The machine supplies nothing this promise can read: a killed or dropped copy leaves bytes at the final name with *"identical mode, permissions, extended attributes, inode flags, allocation and file-type output"* to a whole file, and an interrupted **overwrite** left a **40,000,000-byte hybrid**, *"Neither version, plausible size, no marker"* (Fact 18). **A hybrid is the failure this clause exists to make impossible**, and staging plus a single publication is the only arrangement that does it — never ordering, never care, which is [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#92-three-tests-applied-in-order-and-the-answer-is-declared-rather-than-inferred) §9.2 test 2 stated for this document's own unit.

### 7.2 Where staged material sits, and who names it

**S1. Staged material sits on the same filesystem as the final name, in a Smoozy-named directory beside it.** The requirement is not a preference: publication is a rename, and rename is documented on both ends to require it. Documented — `rename(2)`, macOS 26.5 man page dated June 3, 2021, read 2026-08-17: *"Both old and new must be of the same type (that is, both must be either directories or non-directories) and must reside on the same file system."* Fact 78 quotes the rented end's own manual to the same effect for the atomicity half.

**S2. Placing staging beside the destination closes the container's mount trap by construction.** A container's free-space listing reports the host's disks *"mounted at paths like `/etc/hosts` and the driver binaries"*, and *"Read against the actual output folder"* is the rule Fact 28 draws. Staging beside the destination is on the destination's mount whether or not anything reads a mount list.

**S3.
