Staging never sits under a shared temporary area.** `/tmp` *"is wiped at every boot"* and `/var/tmp` *"is not cleaned at boot and has a documented thirty-day expiry underneath"*, and it *"is the only temporary area that both survives a reboot and sits on disk, which also makes it an easy place to leave one rental's data for the next tenant"* (Fact 6). **The `/var/tmp` words are Fact 6's own corrected wording of 2026-08-20 and are quoted as corrected, said here from 2026-08-23 because the sentence below announces this document's correction of the same claim and a reader could otherwise take the quotation for the original.** **Corrected 2026-08-22: this quoted `/var/tmp` as *never cleaned at all*, which the record it cites had already retracted on 2026-08-20** — a reboot test cannot tell *never* from *after thirty days*, and Ubuntu's shipped `q /var/tmp 1777 root root 30d` sets the expiry. **The rule is unmoved**: an expiry nobody wrote down is a worse place for staging than a wipe that is documented. [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#13-rules-that-bind-every-store) §1.3 rule 2 forbids it for a store; the next-tenant hazard applies to material too, and [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#10-what-smoozy-leaves-on-a-rented-machine) §10 states the same limit from the other side.

**S4. The staging path is synthesised from the transfer identity and the transfer's attempt number, and never from the file's own name.** **The attempt number was added 2026-08-31**, at the first audited reading of this document against [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md): the identity alone was stated as the whole of the synthesis while [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 5 gave one identity a second, fresh staging path, so *the path synthesised from this transfer identity* named two objects and a consumer taking the material up by taking the identity could not tell which. **One path per attempt, one attempt live at a time**: the attempt number starts at one, is carried in [S9](#73-the-progress-record-is-on-the-desktop-side-in-both-directions), is incremented only where a clause of this section starts the transfer over, and the numbers already superseded are retained in the record so the sweep can derive their paths ([S28](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)). This document owns that path, which is the exemption [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#21-the-rules-that-bind-every-identity) §2.1 rule 7 requires to be named — *"no path is ever synthesised from an identity by anything but the one document that owns that path"*. Deriving it from the file's name would put Fact 29's hazards inside the mechanism that is meant to survive them: 33 hostile names reached the machine byte-for-byte, but the Mac collapsed `Capitals.txt` and `capitals.txt` into one file *"one file's name carrying the other's contents, with no error"*, one tool aborted an entire transfer on a non-UTF-8 name where another skipped it, and *"one space in a path breaking three tools three different ways"*.

**S5. No tool ever chooses the staging name.** Fact 17 is exact about the cost of letting one: a hard kill on one box left rsync's own *"randomly named temporary file"* in the destination, *"a name Smoozy cannot predict"*, while the partial directory is reliable *"after an interruption it performs itself"*. Because [8.2](#82-the-enforcement-point-is-smoozys-own-byte-loop-and-the-evidence-forced-it) puts the byte loop inside Smoozy, the name is Smoozy's in every case and the hazard does not arise.

**S5a. Row 281's failure has now been watched happening, by the route that looks safest. Added 2026-08-22.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md) filled a 99 GB filesystem to leave about 41 MiB and pushed a **209 715 200**-byte file into it by both routes:

- **Smoozy's own byte loop into a staging name Smoozy chose** — the write raised at 42 823 680 bytes, the staged file held **41 930 752 bytes** at `route1.bin.smoozy-diskfull.part`, and **the final name was absent.**
- **The standard tool writing straight at the final name** — `scp` exited **1**, printed `scp: write remote "…/route2.bin": Failure`, and left **`route2.bin`, 261 120 bytes of 209 715 200, at the final name.**

**A partial file sitting at the name a user would take for a finished one**, produced by a tool nobody would suspect, on a machine that reported the failure loudly. **This is the strongest single piece of evidence in the stage for [7.5](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step) and for the rule above**, and it is observed rather than argued.

**S6. Staged material is action-held, and its state is not.** The material ends with the action, success or failure ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#111-five-classes-and-the-reason-there-is-no-sixth) §11.1), and its cleanup is this document's under [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends). **The durable record of the transfer is in the local record and on the desktop side in both directions**, and [7.3](#73-the-progress-record-is-on-the-desktop-side-in-both-directions) says why it cannot be anywhere else.

That record's durable home is [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) §1.1's per-transfer records, nested under their transfer arrangement or multi-part action outcome, with [§11.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#111-five-classes-and-the-reason-there-is-no-sixth)'s lifetime — [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 13, which closed the seam on 2026-08-21.

### 7.3 The progress record is on the desktop side, in both directions

**S7. What a transfer has staged, and how far, is recorded in the local record on the user's computer — for an upload as well as for a download.** The reason is a hard one and it is observed: **on a full disk nothing further can be written**, every write refused with `No space left on device` and errno 28 from a shell redirect, from `cp`, and from a flushed and fsynced write (Fact 28). **So a marker written on the machine is exactly what cannot be written in the case it is needed for.** A record on the machine would also be destroyed by a recycle in the case C14 covers, silently and on the same address.

**S8. The record is durable before the first byte is written.** Row 197's third clause is why: an upload cut off by *"the app closing"* keeps its leftover and the user is told it is there. A record written at the end of a transfer is a record a closing process never writes. This is [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#94-the-outcome-record) §9.4's ordering applied here — the enumeration is durable before the first unit is attempted — and [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#3-desired-state-and-observed-state) §3's D6 makes a crash and a clean close leave the same record.

**S9. The record holds, per transfer: the transfer identity, the current attempt number and the attempt numbers already superseded ([S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it)), the direction, the server row and the installation identity it was arranged under, the final path, the staging path, the total size expected, a checkpointed high-water offset with a running digest at each checkpoint, which application run last wrote the record ([C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4), **the staging object's stable file identity recorded beside each attempt number the record retains** — the live attempt's and each superseded attempt's, once that attempt's staging object was created — or, per attempt, the recorded fact that its supplier could not answer ([S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)) — publication-pending evidence ([S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)), and which ending and cleanup disposition of [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) it reached.** It holds no copy of any byte and no observed value the machine still holds.

**Corrected 2026-09-03: the identity was recorded here as one value for the transfer, and the sweep needs one per staging path.** [S28](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s removal requires the path, the synthesised name **and** the stable file identity to match for **each path it derives**, and [S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it) retains the superseded attempt numbers precisely so those paths can be reached — so a single value could satisfy the match for the live attempt and for no other, and every superseded path was unremovable whatever the sweep found there. On a download the user's own computer **can** answer the identity, so this is not [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 15's declared break where a supplier cannot answer: the identity was available and was thrown away when the attempt started over, leaving a part-file inside the user's own destination folder for good on every download that starts over. **It keeps a promise [7.1](#71-the-contract-stated-once) clause 4 already makes rather than changing one**, so it alters nothing the user is given. Found at the code round's first audited reading of this section against the record that performs it, `Audits/Build/Server protocol and transfers/Round 1/Verdict.md`.

**S10. The staged size read from the machine is what the bytes actually are; the record is what was accounted for. Where they differ, neither is taken on trust** — [7.4](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) is the whole of what is done about it.

**S10a. Both clauses are observed, and which way the record is wrong depends on how the transfer ended. Added 2026-08-22.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md), on a 4 GiB transfer held near 384 KB/s so that there was time for it to go wrong:

| How it ended | the record said | the disk actually held | the record was |
|---|---|---|---|
| hard kill at 37 MB | 37 552 128 | 37 847 040 | **294 912 behind** |
| clean stop at 2.14 GB | 2 140 700 672 | 2 140 798 976 | **98 304 behind** |
| **connection killed under a moving transfer**, at 2.30 GB | 2 303 393 792 | 2 303 229 952 | **163 840 ahead** |

**A kill leaves the record behind the disk**, because bytes reached the far end after the last record write. **A dropped connection leaves the record ahead of the disk, because writes are pipelined** ([R6a](#82-the-enforcement-point-is-smoozys-own-byte-loop-and-the-evidence-forced-it)) — the loop counts a chunk as written when it hands it over, and the connection died before some of those chunks landed. **That mechanism is stated nowhere else and it is why [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) takes the smaller of the two and neither on its own.** Had the resume trusted the record after the drop it would have written **163 840 bytes past the end of the staged file**, left a hole, and found out only from the end-to-end digest **after moving the remaining two gigabytes**. **The `min` is not caution; it is the difference between a resume and two gigabytes of wasted transfer.** **And in all three endings the final name was absent** — checked from the machine each time — so part-arrived bytes never sat at the final name. Nothing anywhere reads a staged file's size as a statement of progress, because a zero-byte file *"is indistinguishable between 'still being written', 'the disk filled' and 'the render wrote nothing'"* and two boxes disagreed about whether a refused copy even leaves one (Fact 28).

### 7.4 Resume never trusts a partial it has not verified

**S11. The standard tool's own documentation says why this clause exists, in its own words.** Documented — `sftp(1)`, macOS 26.5, dated December 6, 2024, read 2026-08-17. `reget` is *"Resume download of remote-path. Equivalent to get with the -a flag set"*, and `get -a` is *"attempt to resume partial transfers of existing files. Note that resumption assumes that any partial copy of the local file matches the remote copy. If the remote file contents differ from the partial local copy then the resultant file is likely to be corrupt."* `put -a` and `reput` carry the same warning in the other direction, and the `-a` invocation flag says it outright: *"If the partial contents differ from those being transferred, then the resultant file is likely to be corrupt."* **So resume in both directions exists in the tool present at both ends, and it is unverified by construction.** A corrupt file produced this way carries no error and no marker — which is Row 281's exact failure arriving by the route that looks safest.

**S12. So a resume verifies the staged prefix before it adds a byte, and the verification is against Smoozy's own record rather than against the far end.** In order:

1. **Read the staged size.** Where no staging path exists, the transfer starts from zero.
2. **Take the highest checkpoint in the record at or below that size.** Where there is none, the transfer starts from zero into a fresh staging path.
3. **Compute the digest of the staged prefix up to that checkpoint and compare it with the checkpoint's recorded digest.** After hand-over it is computed at the end the staging path sits on — for an upload's staged prefix, the entry point's framed digest-over-a-byte-range request — and Fact 15 supplies the cost and the tool choice: on a 669,966,622-byte `.blend`, `openssl dgst -sha256` took **0.46 s** against `sha256sum`'s **2.24 s** on the same box — *"The cost is processor, not disk"* — with a cold plain read at 0.34 s, about 1.97 GB/s. **Before hand-over no Smoozy program is on the machine to answer a framed request, so an upload's staged prefix is read back over the subsystem and the digest computed in Smoozy's own loop on the desktop.** The prefix crosses once, downward, and the read-back is cheaper than the start-over it prevents by whatever the line's own asymmetry is. **Two readings of that one line disagree, and the smaller is what this step is priced at — corrected 2026-08-23; this said 11–12 times and cited only the older of the two.** Fact 25 measured **11–12 times** on 2026-08-15/16; [R17](#85-what-is-not-observed-and-it-is-named-here-rather-than-in-a-footnote) measured **about six to one** on 2026-08-22, up 0.62 MB/s against down 3.88. **Six is the factor this comparison uses**, because a comparison priced at the more favourable of two readings of the same line is priced at the one that might not hold. **The conclusion is unmoved at either figure** — a downward crossing at six times the upload rate is still cheaper than re-spending the upload, and the alternative is [S11](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified)'s unverified resume rather than a cheaper verified one. A download's staged prefix is on the user's own computer and is digested there in either phase.

   **What the read-back actually costs was measured on 2026-08-22 and it is linear in what is already staged.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md): a **37 552 128**-byte prefix verified in **6.6 s**, and a **2 140 700 672**-byte prefix in **361.4 s** — both about **5.9 MB/s**. **Six minutes to resume a transfer that was half done.** At that read-back rate, verifying a nearly complete 4 GiB prefix would take **about twelve minutes against a remaining transfer of seconds**, so the cost is highest exactly where the least is left to send. **The comparison this step rests on still holds** — the read-back is cheaper than the start-over by roughly the factor step 3 already states, and the alternative is the unverified resume [S11](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) quotes OpenSSH's own warning about. **What was never written down is that the cost exists at all**, and two things must account for it: **during the verification the transfer moves no bytes and its recorded offset does not change**, so [10.2](#102-what-each-kind-of-work-has-to-report-and-from-where) G4's reporting must not present a resuming transfer as stalled, and no completion estimate may treat a resume as starting at the checkpoint.
4. **Where the prefix verifies, carry on from that checkpoint** and discard whatever sat above it. Bytes between the checkpoint and the staged size are re-sent, which bounds the waste at one checkpoint interval.
5. **Where it does not verify, the attempt number is incremented and the transfer starts over from zero into the staging path that number synthesises** ([S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it)); the superseded attempt's path is abandoned under [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) and its number is retained in the record so the sweep can reach it. It is never repaired in place.

**S12a. A fresh file is not asked what its own request already knows. Added 2026-10-16, on the build runner's ruling R1 after step 10c's owed piece 1.** An upload whose staging folder its own request has just made — that folder's make answered that it made it — and whose record holds no staged part (its first attempt, none superseded, no checkpoint, no staging object ever opened, no publication pending) is not asked for that folder under [S1](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it), its staged size under [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 1, or the removal of whatever stands at its staging name before its staging object is made, because nothing can stand at a name [S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it) synthesised from an identity issued for that file inside a folder made a moment before; **a record with any trace of a staged part takes all three as before, whatever the caller says, and where the exclusive create is refused all the same, the three are made and the create made again.**

**S13. Resuming also requires that the source has not changed under the transfer, and the two directions are answered by different means.** For a file **on the server**, Fact 21 is answered: it can be told not to have changed *"from size, inode, modification time and change time together"*, a save-by-rename changes the inode, and **a hand-restored modification time still moves the change time, which cannot be set back** — and it was posed live, where a byte changed mid-fetch and *"the copy returned 0 and delivered a different file"*, with only the timestamps revealing it. **So all four values are recorded when a download is arranged and all four are compared before a resume**, and any difference starts the transfer over rather than resuming it. **The four behave as this rule assumes on `overlay` too, which was the case the test itself called the least predictable — observed 2026-08-22** ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)): a **rename kept the inode and moved the change time**; a **copy took a new inode**; and a **hand-restored modification time did not move the change time back**. Until then the rule had this from an ordinary filesystem only, and `overlay` is what a container rental's root is.

**One caution about the two time values, on the container kind. Added 2026-08-22.** A container has **no clock of its own** — the reading is the host kernel's, nothing on the machine maintains it, and **nothing inside can correct it** (`Rented-server runtime` V11). **That does not weaken this rule**, because the four values are compared against the four recorded earlier for the same file rather than against any clock, and a host whose clock steps moves both readings together. **What it does forbid is judging a file's age**: no rule here may read a modification or change time as *how long ago* anything happened on a container, and none does. **Which channel supplies them is named here from 2026-08-21, because it was named nowhere and the obvious channel cannot.** **The file-transfer surface carries two of the four and no more:** revision 3's attribute structure defines flags, size, owner and group, permissions, access and modification times, and extensions. **Revision 3 is the revision in play, on two recorded grounds and no longer on a third. Documented:** OpenSSH's own `PROTOCOL` file — the file that opens *"This documents OpenSSH's deviations and extensions to the published SSH protocol."* — read **2026-08-23** at `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openssh/openssh-portable/master/PROTOCOL`, says *"Note that OpenSSH's sftp and sftp-server implement revision 3 of the SSH filexfer protocol described in:"*. **Observed:** protocol version **3** was the version announced by every rental posed — **four boxes across three sellers** ([One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §3.1, 2026-08-17, read raw off the wire as `VERSION packet type=2 version=3`; [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md) group 1, 2026-08-22, machines A, C and D across three sellers, whose surface table reads *"Protocol version announced | 3"*), which is [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 3's widened reading. **Struck 2026-08-23: this clause used to add that the revision was *"confirmed by a session negotiating `Remote version: 3`"*.** **No document under `Preparation/` records that session** — no machine, no end, no client, no date — so it is not an observation this project holds, and it is struck rather than relabelled. [`Dependency Upload & Remap`](Dependency%20Upload%20&%20Remap.md#21-already-on-the-machine) Q5 route 1 carried the same claim, struck it the same way on 2026-08-23, and rests on these same two grounds. **What the observed ground is, exactly: other people's servers.** The four are the sellers' own stock file-transfer servers on rented machines, and [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)'s reading of protocol version 3 was on the caller's own Mac; **nothing Smoozy ships serves this surface in either phase** — the machine's own file-transfer server does ([1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)). **So that a server Smoozy has not yet met announces revision 3 is an expectation rather than an observation**, and where it does not hold, this document's use of revision 3's operations and attribute structure sits on [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 3's per-image ground with the extension set, the operation limits and the session cap, which are read from the server's own opening reply on the first subsystem channel ([1.2](#12-capability-negotiation-and-it-is-load-bearing-rather-than-a-courtesy) item 1) rather than assumed. **No inode, no file identifier, no change time**, and the modification time is a 32-bit count of **seconds**, so no sub-second granularity either. **The change time is the value this rule leans on hardest** — it is what a hand-restored modification time cannot defeat — and it is one of the two the surface does not carry. **So the four-value reading is a command-channel reading** — its path carried under [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)'s admissibility rule, never in the command string — which puts it on [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md#13-unresolved) Section 13 item 9's unestablished ground and is carried there. **Where it is unavailable the resume simply does not happen and the transfer starts over**, which is the same outcome as any difference, so nothing above changes. For a file **on the user's own computer**, the same question is Fact 30's and Row 201's, and `File Browser` owns it. **What is supplied is that a resume of an upload proceeds only where that module's own unchanged-source gates both hold, and that the two ways they can fail have two different outcomes — corrected 2026-08-31; this said that any unconfirmed source starts the transfer over, which is not what that module answers.**

- **Where the source has *changed*, the transfer does not start over: it stops and is reported.** The record is written *stopped* with its reason under [S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s second row, the staged material is removed on that row's rule, and the user is told the file changed while it was being sent and offered the new version. What is said and offered is [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) K10 clause 1's and K8's and is not decided here. **A silent restart is the wrong answer here rather than a lesser one**: it re-sends a whole file over the user's own line for a save the user made, says nothing about the save, and drops the offer Row 201's third clause promises.
- **Where the source is merely unconfirmed without having changed — the staged prefix does not verify by contents and the source's own identity has not moved — the transfer starts over from zero into a fresh staging path under [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 5**, with no claim made about the user's file. **That is a start-over and not an ending**: it is none of S26's five, the transfer is not failed, the carry-on of Rows 199 and 209 is not broken, and the old staging path is abandoned under [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends). The cost is the user's own time, which is the same trade [Section 13](#13-moments-that-reached-the-creator) already records for S12 step 5.

**S14. Resume is exact and it was observed in both directions.** 119,424,000 bytes where 157,286,400 minus 37,862,400 was owed, and 436,786,462 where 669,966,622 minus 233,180,160 was owed, with matching checksums; on a second provider an interrupted transfer resumed in **12.85 seconds against a from-scratch control of 30.97**; and *"The partial can be held off the final name, which is the clause the spec leans on"* (Fact 17). **The two conditions that came with it are why S12 does not lean on a tool**: Apple's `rsync` *"accepted the flag and resumed nothing on the sending direction on one box"*, re-sending in full, and the partial directory is not deterministic after a hard kill.

### 7.5 Publication is the last step, and it is one step

**S15. Publication is a rename of the staging path onto the final path, and it is the only moment the final name changes.** Nothing else in a transfer touches the final name — not a create, not a truncate, not a touch, not a permission change.

**Publication has a durable pre-state, written before the final name is touched.** The transfer record is put into **publication pending**, naming both the staging path and the final path and recording the complete staged object's stable file identity, byte length and SHA-256 digest ([S9](#73-the-progress-record-is-on-the-desktop-side-in-both-directions)). The identity's suppliers are the command-channel reading on the machine and the platform file adapter on the user's computer ([S18](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step) requirement 4). **Where the supplier cannot answer, the record says the identity is unavailable and publication still proceeds on the byte length and digest** — content evidence, which rests on no name — **and every branch below that needed the identity refuses to claim success rather than guessing.** What is never weakened is removal: [S28](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s automatic removal requires the recorded identity and does not run without it. That write is made durable before the first step of publication runs. Four things follow, and between them they close the stretch between *staged complete* and *published*:

- **A record that says *publication pending* does not enter [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) on a resume.** Publication is retried instead. A staged file that is already complete with the final name absent is therefore finished rather than left where it sits.
- **A publication whose staging path no longer exists is not assumed to have happened.** Recovery reads the final object's stable file identity, byte length and SHA-256 digest. Only an exact match with the staged-object evidence durably recorded before rename proves that the expected object crossed and permits the `published` outcome — every value the pre-state recorded, compared; where the identity was recorded unavailable, the byte length and SHA-256 digest are the recorded evidence and both must be read and match, which is content proof resting on no name. Where the proof cannot be composed at all — a recorded value with no route to read its counterpart now — recovery never records `published` and starts a fresh transfer from zero, the mismatch outcome rather than a new one. A missing or different final object records **staging removed outside publication** and starts a fresh transfer from zero; it never converts absence into success. A transfer-aware user deletion has already recorded **staging removed by user** before removing the path, so recovery takes that explicit branch without inspecting the final name. This is what distinguishes a crash after rename from a user deletion or an unrelated cleanup.
- **The successful rename is checked before its outcome is written.** Its final file identity, byte length and digest must equal the publication-pending evidence. The comparison covers what the pre-state recorded and what the routes present can read back — the byte length always, on the file-transfer subsystem; the identity and the at-rest digest where their command-channel or platform routes answer ([S22](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)). A readable value that does not match is a failed publication, not a published file; a value with no route to read it back does not fail the publication — the pre-state already carries the staged object's verified byte length and digest, and the rename is one step ([S16](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)) — and the outcome records which values were compared.
- **It is what makes [S17](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s window recoverable** rather than only declared to be.

**A record whose ending says the work is over holds no publication anybody owes, and its pending mark goes. Added 2026-09-29, the build runner's ruling under the Creator's standing delegation, from step 10c's transfer wave piece 2.** *Failed*, *stalled* and *stopped* ([S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)) are endings no retry follows, so the pre-state above has no reader left but `Download`'s admission, which refuses every download of a server while any of its records holds one (`Download` R6's ordering). **Kept, the mark made one failed publication — the user's own file saved at the name while it downloaded — refuse that server's downloads for good, with nothing said**, which is Smoozy going quiet and which no plan grants. Two limbs, and neither removes anything on a path alone. **At the ending:** the loop writes the mark away in the same write as the ending, **unless Smoozy's own reservation (`Desktop app and delivery` C10) may still stand at the final name** — the platform adapter takes its reservation back when the rename over it fails, and only where the object is still the one it made — and where it may, the mark stays for the next start. **At the next start:** a record already ended that way is settled by `Desktop app and delivery` C13 without completing anything — Smoozy's reservation is removed, anything else at the final name (the user's own file, or nothing) is left exactly as it stands — and the mark then goes; where the final name cannot be read, it stays. A replace-intended pre-state is outside this paragraph.

**S15a. Every publication carries a caller-declared intent, and the intent chooses the operation. Added 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, routed by the runner from [File Browser](File%20Browser.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 12 and [Desktop app and delivery](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#22-unresolved) §22 item 32.**

- **Two intents and no third. Replace-intended**: the caller has established that a file at the final name is to be replaced, and the operation is S16's extension. **Create-intended**: the caller requires that no file at the final name is overwritten, and the operation is the **base rename**, whose refusal of an existing target is exactly the backstop wanted. The intent is a parameter of the publication, declared by the caller and never inferred here.
- **S15's *not a create* is a rule about the transfer, and one caller creates the final name before the transfer under its own contract.** `Desktop app and delivery` C10 reserves a receiving name before the bytes are fetched, to settle a collision on the receiving filesystem while the user is still there to answer. **That is admitted, and it is narrow**: the reservation is a durable, recorded pre-state naming the reserved path; it happens **before** any staging file exists for that transfer, so no part-arrived bytes ever reach the final name and S18's second requirement is untouched; and the publication that follows is **replace-intended over Smoozy's own reservation**, not over a file anybody else put there. **S15's rule is unchanged for everything inside a transfer** — no create, no truncate, no touch, no permission change on the final name between the transfer starting and its publication.
- **Where the caller declares neither, publication does not begin.** There is no default, because the two intents differ in exactly the case that matters and a wrong default is silent in both directions.

**S15b. A publication may carry a caller-declared gate, and the gate is a moment this document names rather than a judgement it makes. Added 2026-09-04 at the code round, routed from [File Browser](File%20Browser.md#93-the-unchanged-file-row-201-and-fact-30) K9, whose no-corruption promise is a comparison this loop had no named point to ask at.**

- **What it is.** The caller supplies a gate with the publication. The loop asks it **once**, at the moment every byte of the transfer has passed and [7.6](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)'s end-to-end comparison has been made, and **before** [S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s durable pre-state is written. It is handed the transfer's own **pass-through digest** ([S22](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)) — the digest of what was actually sent — and returns one of two answers.
- **What this document decides is the moment and nothing else.** What the gate compares that digest against, and what the comparison is worth, is the declaring document's: `File Browser` K9's is a second full reading of the source. **Nothing here reads the caller's file, and no gate of this document's own is composed.**
- **A refusal ends the transfer *stopped: the source changed under it*** — [S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s second row, so the staged material is removed on that row's rule and nothing is published. It is the same ending [S13](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified)'s upload half already takes, and no sixth ending and no second stopped reason is added for it.
- **Why that moment and no other.** Asked earlier, the gate would be handed the pass-through digest of part of the file, which is not the quantity the clause compares. Asked later, the pre-state would already be durable — and a pre-state is precisely what makes a publication recoverable and retried, so a record written past a refusal would be retried past it too.
- **Where the caller declares none, none is asked**, and that is the ordinary case rather than a default standing in for a behaviour: no clause of this document requires a gate, a download has no source on the user's computer to read through, and a transfer without one is unchanged in every respect.
- **It is asked once, and a retried publication does not re-ask it.** [S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s first bullet retries a publication whose pre-state is already durable, at which point this gate's own moment has passed. **What that leaves is said rather than left to be found:** such a retry publishes on the evidence the pre-state carries — the staged object's identity, byte length and digest — and the caller's own read-through is not taken again. Across application runs the case does not arise at all, because [C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4 refuses to resume a record an earlier run wrote.

**S15c. A transfer keeps its source's modification time, set on the staged file before publication. Added 2026-10-04, out of the Creator's note 3 on the test copy, on `Specs/File Browser`'s sentence of that day; no auditor has seen it.** Until then nothing in either direction set a time — the subsystem's set-attributes call was used for permissions alone — so every uploaded file read as changed at the moment it went up.

- **What is set.** On an upload, the staged file on the server takes the local source's modification time as read when the transfer was arranged (`File Browser` K7's path identity). On a download, the staged file on the user's computer takes the server file's modification time as read when it was enumerated or arranged. The access time is set with it, to the same value; nothing else is. **Narrowed 2026-10-08, at the build (the Creator's second notes, piece 10):** an upload with no `File Browser` K7 identity behind it (one that could not be read, or a file Smoozy made itself) takes its source's time by the same reading by path, taken at that moment. A download's time is read **on the handle the transfer opened on the server file**, sent without waiting and answered before the pre-state, because neither the transfer record nor what arranges a download carries an enumerated time, and a separate reading would cost every file a round trip. Where the transfer resumes, S13's comparison has already held the file unchanged, so the two readings agree.
- **By what.** On the machine, the file-transfer subsystem's set-attributes call carrying the times alone — never in the same call as a permission change, which stays its own call. On the user's computer, `Desktop app and delivery`'s platform file adapter.
- **When, and why then.** After the last byte has passed, after [S20](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s flush and after S15b's gate where one is declared, and **before S15's durable pre-state is written.** Setting a time moves a file's change time, and the server-side checks read a file's identity from size, inode, modification time and change time together ([S13](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified); `Render Settings`' reading anchor). Set before the pre-state, the identity the pre-state records, the rename carries and every later reading takes is the identity after the time was set, so **nothing ever records the in-between**, and S15's recovery comparison is unchanged. **Narrowed 2026-10-08, at the build:** on an upload the call is **sent** after the flush, beside the close, without waiting, and its answer is read after the gate and before the pre-state, so it costs no round trip of its own. The gate reads only the user's own file, so a time already on the staged file changes nothing it compares, and a refusal removes the staged file as S26's row says. What this clause binds is unchanged: the time is in place before the pre-state is written.
- **Every route that publishes does it.** The many-small-files route included: where a route publishes through Smoozy's own program on the server rather than through the subsystem, that program sets the time the same way before its own publication.
- **A refusal does not fail the transfer.** Where the call is refused, the file is published as it is and keeps the time it arrived with; nothing else in the contract moves. The time is carried to the precision the call carries.
- **Resume is untouched**: S12 reads the staged size and digest, and the time is set once, at the end.

**S16. On the machine, the operation is the file-transfer subsystem's POSIX-rename extension, and the base operation will not do.** Observed on the Creator's Mac, macOS 26.5, `OpenSSH_10.2p1`, on 2026-08-17, driving OpenSSH's own server subsystem directly over a pipe: `posix-rename@openssh.com` of a 3,145,728-byte staging file onto an existing 1,200,000-byte final file returned status **0, `Success`**; the published file was 3,145,728 bytes, its digest matched the source, **its inode had changed**, and the staging name was gone. The base `SSH_FXP_RENAME` over an existing target returned status **4, `Failure`**. **So the extension is required for an overwrite and the base operation is enough only where the final name does not exist.**

**S17. Where the extension is not announced, a *replace-intended* publication degrades in one declared way and the promise still holds. The scoping to replace-intended was added 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, routed by the runner from [File Browser](File%20Browser.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 12: a create-intended publication never takes this route, because removing the final name first would destroy the very file the intent exists to refuse to overwrite — and it would do so on exactly the machines where the extension is absent, in exactly the two cases the backstop is for.** A create-intended publication uses the base rename in every case, announced extension or not, and its refusal of an existing target is the answer rather than a fault. The degradation below is replace-intended only. The staging file is created, the final name is removed, and the base rename is performed. **No part-arrived bytes ever reach the final name and no hybrid is ever produced**, so Row 281 is kept. What is lost is the replacement's indivisibility: there is a window in which neither the previous file nor the new one is at the final name. **That window is recoverable and not a loss**, because the staged file is whole, S9's record names both paths, and [S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s pre-state is written before the removal — so the removal is a deliberate recorded act rather than a silent one, and a connection that meets the final name absent knows Smoozy removed it and retries publication rather than reading the absence as ordinary. It is a real difference and it is declared here rather than discovered. Nothing else is offered as a fallback, and in particular nothing writes to the final name and hopes. **The compound case — a machine announcing neither the POSIX-rename extension nor the fsync extension, so that this route is taken while [S20](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s durability ordering does not apply — is [Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 7's, because Fact 44 is what decides it.**

**How likely this branch is at all was established on 2026-08-21, and the answer is that no machine this project can meet takes it.** `posix-rename@openssh.com` has been announced since OpenSSH 4.9. **Documented** — OpenSSH's own release announcement for that release, read 2026-08-23 at `https://www.openssh.com/txt/release-4.9`: *"OpenSSH 4.9 was released on 2008-03-31"*, and under **New features**, *"Added a protocol extension method "posix-rename@openssh.com" for sftp-server(8) to perform POSIX atomic rename() operations. (bz#1400)"*. **The address and the quoted words are new to this document as of 2026-08-23; the claim stood with neither, here and in every other place this project makes it.** The oldest server on record here is 8.9p1; the one rental interrogated announced it, with the version byte the specification names ([Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 3, Fact 93). **The release announcement is what carries the version history and OpenSSH's `PROTOCOL` file does not** — `PROTOCOL` specifies what the extension performs and names no release it first appeared in, at master and at the 8.9p1 tag alike (both read 2026-08-23). **S17 stays written and stays unchanged** — it costs nothing, it is the honest answer if a server ever does withhold the extension, and an absent extension is still an announcement Smoozy reads rather than a thing it assumes. What changes is only that a builder should not treat this route as an ordinary one to be exercised.

**S18. On the user's computer, the operation is `Desktop app and delivery`'s platform file adapter and the contract on it is stated here.** Four requirements: the staged file appears at the final name in **one** step; **no part-arrived bytes ever appear at the final name**; staging is on the same filesystem as the final name (S1); and **the adapter supplies the staged and final objects' stable file identity where the platform has one** — [S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s pre-state, recovery and rename check and [S28](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s removal match read it, and an adapter that cannot answer is met by S15's identity-unavailable branch rather than by a guess. What the operation is on each of Mac, Windows and Linux, and its demonstration there, is that document's under Fact 79 — the shape says so: *"`Desktop app and delivery` supplies the platform-file operation to `Server protocol and transfers`."*

**S19. What the Mac's own manual says about that operation, recorded because it moves Fact 79 and the register does not hold it.** Documented — `rename(2)`, macOS 26.5, dated June 3, 2021, read 2026-08-17: *"The rename() system call guarantees that an instance of new will always exist, even if the system should crash in the middle of the operation."* That is a crash guarantee in the vendor's own words and it is the Mac third of Fact 79's first half. It says nothing about the durability of the staged file's **contents** before the rename, which is what S20 is for, and it says nothing about Windows or about a desktop Linux computer, neither of which this project can reach.

**S20. The staged bytes are made durable before publication, where the server offers it.** Observed on the Creator's Mac, macOS 26.5, `OpenSSH_10.2p1`, on 2026-08-17, over a pipe to OpenSSH's own server subsystem: `fsync@openssh.com` against an open handle returned status **0, `Success`**, and the subsystem announces it. Documented — `sftp(1)`: `-f` *"Requests that files be flushed to disk immediately after transfer. When uploading files, this feature is only enabled if the server implements the 'fsync@openssh.com' extension."* **This is ordering rather than atomicity**: it makes the staged content durable before the name that publishes it exists, so a machine that loses power after publication cannot present a published name over undurable bytes. It does not close Fact 44, which is not posable on any machine this project can reach.

**S20a. What that flush covers is the staged file and nothing else, and the bound is stated here rather than left as a silence. Added 2026-09-03 at the code round, beside [Job files and outputs](Job%20files%20and%20outputs.md#34-the-attempt-records-beside-it) M8's repair of the same gap.** The operation takes an **open file handle**, so what it makes durable is the object that handle names. **The directory that afterwards holds the published name is not flushed** — in either direction, for any publication under this contract — **and nothing in this project has established that it can be over this subsystem**: the subsystem exposes no operation that opens a directory and hands back a handle, and whether a server would answer a flush against such a handle has been read nowhere here. **No route to it is written from remembered behaviour**, and the unread question is carried in this document's sources file as a *no source*.

**What holds without that part.** S20's own ordering, which keeps a published name from ever standing over undurable bytes. [S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s durable pre-state, written before the final name is touched and naming both paths with the staged object's identity, byte length and digest — so a publication that did not survive is retried, and its proof is composed from content rather than from the final name being present. And [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified), which verifies a staged prefix rather than trusting a record's number.

**What it costs, and no number is written for it.** A published name can be absent afterwards on a machine whose directory entry never reached the disk; what that costs a job record and an attempt record is priced in [Job files and outputs](Job%20files%20and%20outputs.md#34-the-attempt-records-beside-it) M8, which takes this contract entire. **Nothing in this project's plans establishes how long an unflushed directory entry survives, on any machine or any filesystem**, so no figure is stated here and none is inferred — Fact 44 is what would decide it and it is not posable ([Section 12](#12-unresolved) item 7).

### 7.6 Integrity: what is compared, and when

**S21. Two different things protect two different stretches, and neither covers the other.**

- **The transport covers the pipe, for free and hard.** With a relay flipping exactly one bit and a control run first proving the relay faithful, the connection **died in both directions** — `message authentication code incorrect` downloading, `Broken pipe` uploading (Fact 72). **Nothing is added to detect damage in flight.** What is added is that the failure is read correctly: it presents as a dropped connection, so **a dropped channel is never read as a clean ending** and [7.8](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) treats it as an interruption.
- **An end-to-end digest covers everything the pipe does not**: the file at rest before it was read, the staged bytes at rest, and a resumed prefix. Fact 72 is why it cannot be skipped — four bytes overwritten in place with the modification time restored travelled through `scp` at exit 0, and a default `rsync` *"called it done: 48 bytes sent, exit 0, file still corrupted"*, and *"with a change summary requested it printed nothing at all"*.

**S22. The pass-through digest is computed in Smoozy's own byte loop, over the bytes as they pass, and it is never asked of a tool.** Two reasons and both are observed. The tool sets differ at the two ends and the odd end is the user's: *"`sftp`'s 33 commands include **no checksum, verify, hash or digest command of any kind**, and `scp` has nothing either"*, while Apple's `rsync` does support checksum comparison and Apple's `rsync` is what a Mac ships (Fact 72). And computing as the bytes pass costs no second read of the file, which is the cost Fact 15 measures. **The loop is on the caller's side** ([R5](#82-the-enforcement-point-is-smoozys-own-byte-loop-and-the-evidence-forced-it)): before hand-over there is no Smoozy component on the machine at all ([1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one)), so there is no second loop on the machine for this digest to be computed in, and this clause claims no such thing.

**A digest of material at rest on the machine is a different request, and it has one route per phase.** After hand-over it is the framed digest over a named byte range that [1.1](#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) item 1 names and C6 classes as a reading, answered through the entry point by a program the machine carries. Before hand-over it is taken by reading the bytes back over the subsystem into Smoozy's own loop — no machine program and no command ([S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 3, [B10](#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection)). Either way it is what [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 3 needs for an upload's staged prefix and what [S21](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)'s *staged bytes at rest* stretch is covered by. Its value is read **as a value and never as a cause**, which [1.3](#13-what-the-surface-does-not-carry) and Fact 37 already bind. **And it fails safe:** a digest that cannot be read, or that is read and does not match, starts the transfer over from zero into a fresh staging path under [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 5 rather than publishing.

**S23. One digest function per protocol version, the same at both ends, and its implementation is chosen rather than inherited.** Fact 15 measured SHA-256 at both ends — 2.24 s on the machine and 2.22 s on the Mac for a 669,966,622-byte file, and **0.46 s on the same machine with a different implementation of the same function**. So the function is fixed by the protocol version and the implementation is picked for speed at each end, *"on processors with the instructions that make it fast, which both ends here had and a rental may not"*.

**S24. Where the digests disagree, the transfer ends *failed* ([S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)) and the staged material is abandoned.** It is never published, never repaired in place, never resumed ([S26b](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)), and never retried inside the action ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#95-no-unit-is-retried-inside-its-action) §9.5). Trying again is a new transfer with a new identity.

**S25. A digest computed as the bytes pass proves the bytes read match the bytes written, and no more.** It does not prove the file on the sending disk was whole before Smoozy read it. That is what S13's change test covers on the server side and Row 201's on the local side, and it is stated here so no reader takes the digest for more than it is.

### 7.7 What a transfer does not do on its own, which is the answer to the second clause

**Row 281's second clause asks whether keeping the promise needs any work beyond what a transfer already does on its own. It does, and this is the list.**

| What the promise needs | Does a transfer do it on its own? |
|---|---|
| Part-arrived bytes kept off the final name | **No.** In both directions a killed or dropped copy leaves them at the final name with nothing to distinguish them, and an interrupted overwrite leaves a plausible-sized hybrid (Fact 18). **Added: S1 to S6, and S15.** |
| A part file told from a whole one afterwards | **No.** Nothing on the machine distinguishes them — identical mode, permissions, extended attributes, inode flags, allocation and file-type output (Fact 18). **Added: S7 to S10, the record, on the desktop side in both directions.** |
| Damage in flight detected | **Yes**, hard, by the transport (Fact 72). **Added only: reading the drop as an interruption rather than an ending.** |
| Damage in flight told from an ordinary drop | **No.** It presents as a dropped connection and the machine's own log says nothing (Fact 72). **Added: nothing — it is not attempted, and S21 says why it need not be.** |
| Damage at rest detected | **No.** Invisible to `scp` and to a default `rsync` (Fact 72). **Added: S21 to S25.** |
| A resumed partial verified before it is added to | **No**, and OpenSSH says so in its own words — resumption *assumes* the partial matches and *"the resultant file is likely to be corrupt"* if it does not. **Added: S11 to S14.** |
| A source that changed under the transfer caught | **No.** A byte changed mid-fetch and *"the copy returned 0 and delivered a different file"* (Fact 21). **Added: S13.** |
| The partial found again after a hard kill | **No.** One box left a randomly named temporary file Smoozy cannot predict (Fact 17). **Added: S4 and S5 — Smoozy names the path, so the case does not arise.** |
| Publication kept off the final name until the end | **No.** The base protocol rename refuses an existing target and every ordinary tool writes straight to the final name (observed 2026-08-17; Fact 18). **Added: S15 to S20.** |

**So the answer to the clause is: nearly all of it is added work, and one line of it is free.** That is why the contract is written once here and cited by seven module documents rather than restated in each.

**S31. Nothing is compressed, in either direction, at either layer — and the second half of that is a requirement rather than a preference. Added 2026-08-31 for Row 514.** The row is the half of a promise this stage split from its sibling and never carried; [Open Threads](../Groundwork/Open%20Threads.md)'s transfers thread rules it Architecture's in as many words, and this document's own table above never mentioned it either, so a reader looking for the negative did not find one.

- **The connection is not compressed.** Compression is a per-connection setting the transport already offers and it is **off by default at the client**. Documented — `ssh_config(5)`, `Compression`: *"Specifies whether to use compression. The argument must be yes or no (the default)."*; `sshd_config(5)`, `Compression`: *"Specifies whether compression is enabled after the user has authenticated successfully. The argument must be yes, delayed (a legacy synonym for yes) or no. The default is yes."* — so the far end permits it and the client decides. It is left at the client default, on the transport's own advice in its own words: `ssh(1)`, `-C`, *"Compression is desirable on modem lines and other slow connections, but **will only slow down things on fast networks.**"* All three read 2026-08-31 from OpenSSH 10.3p1's manual pages as shipped on the Creator's Mac, macOS 26.6, dated March 23, 2026. **And it would buy little in any case**, because nearly everything crossing here is compressed before it starts: a Blender file saves compressed from 5.0 on ([`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) L8), an artist's assets are already-compressed picture formats, and finished frames come back as the same.
- **The file body is never compressed, and this is the requirement.** A checkpoint is a **byte offset into the staged file** and its digest covers that file's own bytes ([S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) steps 1 to 3). Sending a compressed body and unpacking it at the far end would make a resumed offset an offset into a compressed stream, so **a resumed offset would mean something different from a fresh one**, S12's prefix digest would cover bytes that are not the file's, and [S13](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified)'s four-value unchanged-source reading would describe something other than what crosses. **So the staged bytes and the wire bytes are the same bytes**, in both directions, before and after hand-over. Any later proposal to compress a body is a change to this clause and to S12 together, never to one of them.
- **What this does not reach.** [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) L9 already undoes a Blender file's own compression to read its version out of a fetched prefix. That is reading a file's contents, not squeezing a transfer, and it is untouched here.

### 7.8 Cleanup, and the five ways a transfer ends

**S26. A transfer ends in exactly one of five ways, the record says which, and the five do different things with the staged material.** Row 197 requires the difference and the specs state it for the upload direction; the contract holds it in both. **Raised from three to five on 2026-08-31**, at the first audited reading of this document against [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md), whose finding S26a's first bullet is the correction of.

| Ending | Staged material | May the work be carried on | Record |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Published** | gone, taken by the rename itself (observed: the staging name was gone) | no — the transfer reached the final name | the outcome is written durably before it is presented ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#94-the-outcome-record) §9.4) |
| **Stopped, deliberately** | **removed at once**, in the same connection the stop was issued on where it is still up, and otherwise on the next connection to that server as an owed cleanup | no | the record says *stopped* and its reason — including **staging removed by user** where `File Browser` initiated it, and **abandoned by its request** where the module that arranged it abandons the staging instead of taking it up ([S28](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)) — and is written **before** the channel is closed or path is removed (C23) |
| **Failed** — the row names its reason rather than being defined by one, and there are three: **the digests disagree at the end** ([S24](#76-integrity-what-is-compared-and-when)); **a publication check that came back wrong** ([S15](#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step)'s third bullet, a readable value that does not match); and **a local failure on the user's own computer that a reconnection cannot clear** — the staging file that cannot be made, written to, read back or published on the user's own disk. **Widened 2026-09-03**; this row read *the digests disagree at the end* and was defined by that one reason. | **removed**, on the *stopped* row's rule: at once where the connection is still up, otherwise as an owed cleanup under [S27](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) | **no** — resuming over material S24 has abandoned would resume over bytes already known to be wrong, and neither of the other two reasons clears by being tried again on a fresh connection | the record says *failed* and its reason — a digest mismatch, a publication check that failed, or a local failure a reconnection cannot clear — and is written before the channel is closed or the path is removed (C23) |
| **Stalled** — the transfer channel's inactivity deadline expired ([C19](#46-per-waiter-deadlines)) | **removed**, on the same rule as *failed* | **no** — nothing resumes it, and trying again is a new transfer with a new identity | the record says *stalled*. It is a statement about the channel and never about the connection ([G8](#103-a-slow-thing-and-a-stopped-one)) |
| **Interrupted** — a drop, a damaged pipe, the process ending | **kept**, so it can be carried on | **yes**, under [7.4](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) and [C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4 | the record says *interrupted* and names the staging path and the last checkpoint |

**S26a. Three things about that table. Added 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, routed by the runner; the first bullet reversed 2026-08-31 and the third added 2026-09-03.**

- **An integrity failure is *failed*, a fourth ending of its own. Reversed 2026-08-31 at the first audited reading of this document against [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md); this bullet used to make it *interrupted*.** S24 abandons staged material whose digest disagrees, which was none of the three as the table stood. Recording it as *interrupted* put it under the one word that means *this may be carried on*, and cost two things at once: [C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4 picked it up at the next repair and, its staging having been removed, [S12](#74-resume-never-trusts-a-partial-it-has-not-verified) step 1 re-sent the whole file with nothing said; and `File Browser` U5 showed it as *waiting for the connection* on a live and healthy line. **What a reader needs from an ending is what became of the final name and whether the work may be carried on**, and those are two answers rather than one — so the second is a column of the table and an ending that answers it differently is its own row. Its reason is recorded as a digest mismatch and its staged material is removed rather than kept. Routed from [Download](Download.md#15-unresolved) §15 item 20.
- **The second row's *deliberately* means *on a deliberate instruction*, and the instruction need not be the user's.** Smoozy's own arrests write that row — `File Browser`'s K8 and K9 and X7, and `Download`'s P6 for a left-alone publication refusal — and each is a deliberate act of Smoozy's rather than of the user's. **The row's substance does not move**: the staged material is removed at once or as an owed cleanup, the record says *stopped* and its reason, and the reason is what names who stopped it. Routed from [File Browser](File%20Browser.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 12 and [Download](Download.md#15-unresolved) §15 item 21.
- **The *failed* row names its reason, and two more reasons join the digest mismatch. Added 2026-09-03, from the code round's first audited reading of this section against the byte loop that performs it.** The five endings stay five and the row's material rule is already right for all three. **What was wrong is that the row was defined by the one reason it had**, so two failures that will never clear had nowhere else to go and were being written down as *interrupted*: a **publication whose check came back wrong**, and a **local failure on the user's own disk**. **That is the same cost the first bullet reversed, arriving by two other doors** — [C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4 picks an *interrupted* record up at the next repair and retries it, and `File Browser` U5 shows it as *waiting for the connection* on a live and healthy line. A publication that landed and was not recorded would be retried for ever against a staging file the rename already took away; a user whose disk is full would be shown a connection problem, would check their internet and restart their router, and would never be told the one thing they could act on. **The alternative was to leave both *interrupted* on the ground that a full disk may clear later**, and it is refused: that keeps Smoozy quiet about the thing the user can fix, and telling the truth about what happened is never past the line [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) draws. **This is a change to what the user gets** — a transfer that cannot be carried on stops being shown as waiting for a connection, and its leftover material is removed rather than kept — and it was ruled under the Creator's standing delegation of 2026-08-31, repeated 2026-09-03, at `Audits/Build/Server protocol and transfers/Round 1/Verdict.md`.

**S26b. *Interrupted* is the only ending that means the work is waiting to be carried on, and nothing may present any other ending as waiting. Added 2026-08-31.** Every consumer that reads an ending reads that column: [C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4 resumes *interrupted* and no other ending, and no composed presentation may show a *failed* or a *stalled* transfer as waiting for a connection — on both endings the connection is up and the work is over. This is what [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) U5's *waiting for the connection* composes over, and its exclusion list is the four endings that are not *interrupted* rather than the two that document writes itself.

**S26c. *Interrupted* in this document names a transfer's ending and nothing else, and the identical word on a render attempt is an unrelated mark. Added 2026-09-02.** Everything this document says about the word — [S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s row, [S26b](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s exclusivity, [S28a](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s ordering, [C11](#44-re-establishing-after-a-connection-servers-replaced) step 4's resume and [S30](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)'s refusal to infer cleanup — binds a **transfer record** and reaches no other record anywhere. **[`Queue & History`](Queue%20&%20History.md) N1's *interrupted and re-run* is a mark on a render attempt in the per-job note**, and it is not this ending, not derived from it, and not a consumer of it: nothing here writes it, nothing here reads it, and a render attempt marked interrupted says nothing about any transfer's staged material. **The two senses meet in one paragraph and a reader will meet them there** — `Server Setup` T7 says a transfer in flight dies with the machine and its record says *interrupted*, and in the same paragraph says a running render dies with it too — so the sense is fixed by the record the word sits on and never by the sentence around it. **The user meets the render sense** in the catch-up notice `Queue & History` composes; **a builder meets the transfer sense** in S26's table. **What may not be done:** no module may resume a transfer from a render attempt's mark, mark a render attempt from a transfer's ending, or compose one presentation over both.

**S27. A stop that could not be carried out is an owed cleanup and not a forgotten one.** The pattern is not invented here: it is the recorded debt [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#51-the-order-the-recorded-debt-and-what-a-crash-between-the-steps-leaves) §5.1 uses for a secret and [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#101-the-history-tombstone) §10.1 uses for a machine-side history copy, applied a third time so a builder meets one shape rather than three. The debt is cleared only when the removal is confirmed.

**S28. Abandoned staging is removed by a record-driven sweep in each direction.** A sweep begins from a live or ended transfer record in an explicit removable state — **stopped, failed, stalled, superseded by an S12 step 5 start-over, staging removed by user, abandoned by its request, or installation-replaced** — and derives the exact staging path from that record's transfer identity, the attempt number the path was synthesised under ([S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it)) and its recorded installation identity. **Three of those seven were added or made reachable on 2026-08-31**: *failed* and *stalled* are the two endings [S26](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends) separated out of *interrupted* that day; *abandoned by its request* is below; and *superseded by an S12 step 5 start-over* was on this list already with no way to derive the superseded path, which S4's attempt number now supplies. It removes the object only when the path, synthesized name and stable file identity all match the record. **A path whose record is gone is an orphan and is not automatically removed; a path found by enumerating a staging directory is never ownership evidence.** A path with a different file identity is left alone and reported as an ownership mismatch. Neither sweep ever reaches outside the directories [S4](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it) names. **There are two directions because the trigger that reaches one does not reach the other**, and 7.1 clause 4 holds in both.

**S28a. An *interrupted* record is never swept, so a module that abandons its staging writes the record to *stopped* first. Added 2026-08-31.** *Interrupted* means the work may be carried on ([S26b](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends)) and its material is kept for exactly that, so it is on no removable state and the sweep does not reach it — correctly, because a sweep that removed it would remove the leftover Row 197 promises the user. **The consequence is a requirement on whoever decides not to carry it on.** Where the module that arranged a transfer abandons an *interrupted* transfer's staging rather than taking it up — [`File Browser`](File%20Browser.md) U8 does this at a declined take-up and at an ordinary **Skip existing**, and `Download` at the same shape — **it first writes that transfer record durably to *stopped*, with the reason *abandoned by its request*, and only then arranges the replacement unit.** That is the ordering U8 already uses for a user-initiated removal, and it is what puts the material on this sweep's list; without it an ordinary *Skip existing* leaves every leftover partial on the rental for the life of the installation, which is the accumulation [Section 13](#13-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records this sweep as existing against. **Nothing infers the abandonment from the absence of a take-up**: the record is written before the replacement is arranged, or the material is not swept.

- **On the machine.** The sweep runs at the next connection to that server. **It must complete before any transfer on that connection reuses a swept staging path**, and otherwise it runs beside admission rather than in front of it. It is none of [G4](#102-what-each-kind-of-work-has-to-report-and-from-where)'s three kinds of work and has nothing to report while it runs, so it never stands between the user's click and the first byte, which [G1](#101-the-rule-put-as-a-requirement-on-every-request-this-document-defines) would make a fault.
- **On the user's own computer.** A download's staging directory sits inside the user's own destination folder ([S1](#72-where-staged-material-sits-and-who-names-it)), and a trigger that requires a connection to a particular server does not reach material on the user's disk once that server is gone. **So the desktop-side sweep runs at application start, before any transfer is admitted**, beginning from removable transfer records and applying S28's exact three-part match. **Orphans without records remain visible for the user's explicit removal through [`Download`](Download.md); the sweep does not claim them.** **Corrected 2026-08-31: this said `File Browser`, which browses remote machines only and disclaims every local surface, so this material had no owner at all.** `Download` is the module that creates a download's local staging inside the user's own destination folder, and the orphan is that staging; the machine-side orphan route stays `File Browser` X12's, which is a surface on a rented machine. What the local route looks like to the user is `Download`'s and `Desktop app and delivery`'s and is not decided here.
- **When a server row is removed.** Before the row's transfer records are released, each matching local staged object is removed and that cleanup is durably acknowledged. A removal that cannot complete leaves the minimal transfer cleanup-debt record of [S27](#78-cleanup-and-the-five-ways-a-transfer-ends), holding the former server and installation identities only until the exact object is removed or the user explicitly dismisses the unreachable debt. Who removes a server row is `Servers`'; what becomes of this document's staged material at that moment is this document's.

**S29. Nothing about cleanup reads a file's presence, directory membership or size as ownership or state.** [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#13-rules-that-bind-every-store) §1.3 rule 5, and S10's reason. A missing record proves nothing about material at a path; automatic removal requires S28's record, exact synthesized identity and stable-file-identity match.

**S30. Cleanup is never inferred from a machine being reachable again.** [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#10-what-smoozy-leaves-on-a-rented-machine) §10 states the general rule and the recycle observation is the reason. Material a recycle destroyed is gone, and the record still says *interrupted* until C14's branch disqualifies it.

---

## 8. Per-channel rate enforcement

**Constraint:** Rows 289 and 306, and Fact 75, quoted in *What binds this*. `Desktop app and delivery` holds the one shared upload budget and the one shared download budget across every server and channel ([The shape](The%20shape.md#7-desktop-app-and-delivery)).

### 8.1 Where the budget is and where the enforcement is

**R1. There is one upload budget and one download budget for the whole application, and neither is in this document.** They are `Desktop app and delivery`'s single desktop-side scheduler's. **This document does not hold a second budget, does not divide one, and does not cache one.**

**R2. What is here is per-channel enforcement: each transfer channel's byte loop draws from the shared budget on each accounting interval and moves no more than it drew.** So the total across every channel to every server is the budget, which is Row 289's *total across every transfer running at that moment* and *across every server at once* by construction rather than by arithmetic.

**R3. A per-channel or per-transfer limit is forbidden.** Row 289's own words rule it out — *"not a limit applied to each one"* — and Fact 75 shows what it costs even when the numbers add up: a two-way 400+400 split against an 800 KB/s budget achieved **421.9 KB/s**, *"half the budget forfeited the moment the small transfer finished."*

### 8.2 The enforcement point is Smoozy's own byte loop, and the evidence forced it

**R4. The enforcement point cannot be an external tool's flag, and this is settled by measurement and by documentation rather than by preference.** Fact 75: *"the limit is **per invocation**, so dividing a total budget statically only holds while every transfer is still running"*, and one shared budget in one process on the same work achieved **820 KB/s** against 421.9 and cut 21.85 seconds to 11.24 — *"So a total cap needs one shared budget, not per-transfer caps."* Documented, from the standard tool's own manual — `sftp(1)`, macOS 26.5, dated December 6, 2024, read 2026-08-17: `-l limit` *"Limits the used bandwidth, specified in Kbit/s."* **It is an invocation flag with no route to change it while the invocation runs**, so the external-tool route fails Row 306 by construction and not merely in measurement.

**R5. So the bytes move through Smoozy, and that is observed to be possible.** Driven directly against OpenSSH's own file-transfer server subsystem on 2026-08-17: writes at caller-chosen byte offsets in caller-chosen chunks landed at those offsets, a transfer deliberately stopped at 1,048,576 bytes left a staging file of exactly that size with the final name absent, a second open of the same path without truncation carried on from byte 1,048,576, and the finished 3,145,728-byte file's digest matched the source. **A negative result would have been a write at a non-zero offset refused, or silently landing at zero, or a resumed file whose digest did not match**; none happened. The server's own announced limits bound the chunk: **max-write 261,120 bytes**.

**R6. The accounting interval is bounded in both quantities, and a byte bound alone will not do.** The write chunk is the largest write, chosen inside the negotiated limit ([1.2](#12-capability-negotiation-and-it-is-load-bearing-rather-than-a-courtesy) item 2) — that is the byte half. **The time half is the one that matters for R8 and G4:** a chunk bounds bytes, and the delay between two chunks is bytes divided by the rate the channel is actually drawing, which R2 makes the shared budget divided across every admitted channel. At 261,120 bytes, a 50 KB/s cap and four channels, one chunk is about 21 seconds, so a byte bound is no bound on time at all — a raised cap would appear to do nothing for that long, and a transfer would report an unmoving number for that long. **So the loop consults the budget and emits its count at least once per a fixed short period whatever the chunk size, and it sizes each write to the allowance it drew rather than always to the full chunk.** Together those make the budget consulted many times per second per channel rather than once per transfer, and they are the whole mechanism behind [8.3](#83-a-cap-reaches-transfers-already-running-which-is-row-306).

**R6a. The write path is pipelined and is never synchronous per chunk, and this is a requirement rather than an optimisation.
